  there." **Attention, not fear:** "Not yet." / "If you see the one you hit, send the boy to me in the line. Stay among the women." / "The
  boy does as he's told." (Felix later vaults the wall after the bandaged boy). At the altar "Valens kept his grip on the horns until it was
  over." **The inside, as a bodyguard saw it** (told in Kallia's court "with a bowl of her lentils going cold on his knee"): the door and
  the names ("Praxias son of Kleon, of Delphi, for the house of Kallia. He gave it out twice, the second for the Thessalians' benefit.");
  **mind-reading, wrong in a mundane way:** the short-sighted Euthydikos "put his nose a hand from mine to make me out. He was slow about
  it, and he knew the face." (Mara: "He puts his nose that close to everything,"); **the carrier's name: "I gave him Felix."** (CH11 open
  item: shown without a gloss; the priest's wax reads "Maira, of Syracuse, of the house of Kallia, a kinswoman"); the porch writing ("Big.
  Men stop at it and move their lips."; he pretends nothing, Mara answers Felix, and there is no gaze beat); smoke, laurel and barley on the
  hearth; "The floor slopes to the back, not much, but a man with bad feet would feel it."; "There's one door. I looked for another."; the
  bench, the rods, the praying Boeotian, the iron chair ("He said a chair was a chair."); the stone: "Stone color," … "and it came to my
  waist." … "A man was posted beside it watching the bench from start to finish, and I didn't care for him, so I watched him." … "He had a
  cough."; the screen, where the priest "reads the question off the wax, at the screen, in an ordinary voice"; the voice described, never
  quoted ("a woman, not loud"; the break; "Hoarse. Higher than I'd have put her." / "Tired."); **a word not Greek, twice, which he did not
  keep** ("I didn't keep it. It wasn't in what he gave me." / "It's gone,"); the priest's raised flat hand, shown with his own scarred hand;
  "Hers were fewer; that much was plain. He listened to all of hers, and after it he gave me his."; **the question he would not put:** "I
  told him the goat was hers." and, of Praxias in the porch, "I told him it was hers." **Out of the temple:** "Not here." / "At Kallia's."
  (his one walk-off), then silent "the length of the Way". **The anchor:** stands unasked in the middle of the court, feet set, weight even,
  "his hands open at his sides, and fixed on the wall above the woodstack", and gives the answer once, word for word, "in a voice that was
  new to me"; asked for the mountain part alone, he cannot start in the middle ("his hand lifted an inch from his knee"; "A trainer at the
  school called the forms. Eight or ten cuts at a breath" … "Get one wrong and you started again from the first. You don't go in at the
  fourth." / "I can't take it from the sea onward. I'd have to start at the beginning."). **The priest's gloss, passed on unjudged:** "He
  said that was a fine answer for a Syracusan and I was to tell her so. I'm telling you." **Questions about her country:** "Is your country
  west?" / "Where do they put the dead, there?" / "Inside the walls?" / "Where does the town stop, then?" (answered: "Where a board by the
  road says it does,"). **To the riddle:** "It's what he said." / "Then it's something." / "You'll try the cave again," / "It's shut." **To
  Felix's Capua:** no word; "Valens rose and took his bowl to the lean-to, and put it in Kallia's hands." / "The string's had no water since
  daybreak," (Felix does not hear "the school"). "The boy wanted it written down somewhere." / "Mine's written. At Corinth." (CH12-8 NOT
  resolved: which name is written stays open). **Kallia keeps him the night:** "The string, though." / "Your house is full to the roof."
  (her "There's floor," closes it; Ch.14 shows him across the doorway). **Watches the street:** "There he set his shoulders to the wall with
  the whole climb of the street in front of him"; "The house with the crows on it," … "Just under the top." No comparison in his speech;
  **bare "No." 1** (to "Did the priest give you anything about when?"); narrated non-answers 0; walk-offs 1; no watch (none until Ch.18); no
  knot near his hands. **Spent in Ch.13:** the wreath as if put there while he slept; nose-to-nose mind-reading; "I gave him Felix."; "Stone
  color"; the man with the cough; "There's one door. I looked for another."; the word he didn't keep; the raised flat hand; "the goat was
  hers"; the refusal by the hands (did not touch); standing to recite; the forms called by a trainer (said once; never his pressure device);
  "Where does the town stop, then?"; "Mine's written."

- **Ch.14 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; mostly asleep. SYNCED 2026-10-20: FINAL page, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ7; every quote below is on
  the page, checked by script):** at the corner "Valens was still leaning on the same wall. He pushed himself upright when he saw me." /
  "She kept you." (Mara: "Theano's jar was empty."); "He gave the crows' house above us one look."; walks "on the house side, half a step
  behind". **Asleep across the storeroom door** on the blanket Kallia laid ("crosswise on the threshold"), in his cloak, facing out, "the
  bruised arm uppermost", breathing "very quiet for so large a man". **Wakes at her laugh, armed before awake:** "On his blanket Valens was
  up on one elbow. He had moved in silence." / "The knife from the road was out of his belt and lying back along his forearm, point to the
  elbow. He was facing the court and the gate and the black beneath the gallery. He spoke under the sound of the Thessalians' breathing." /
  "Was that you?" / "He left the knife where it was." (FZ2: the knife stays out through the question) / after the well story, "The knife
  went back into his belt." / "The moon's down by midnight," … "She'll dig it crooked." (practical, the sky and the ground, an unintended
  joke; no comparison; the moon agrees with the 7th of Bysios, continuity C-01) / "He lay down again, facing out, and pulled the cloak up
  over his ear. He slept almost at once." **Deliberation pause for Valens: 0** (the draft's weighing beat is cut). **Gaze-holding frame: 0**
  (FZ2 cut the one C2 had brought in). Mara's narration of what he would ask if told ("where it was and how far, and whether the road was
  good, and how the ground lay round it") is his attention axis seen from outside. When she steps over him he "slept on, or let me think he
  did". **Before light he has gone down for the mules**, leaving word with Kallia (Ch.13's "You'll try the cave again" honored as an act).
  Bare "No." 0; narrated non-answers 0; walk-offs 0 on the page; no knot; no watch. **Spent in Ch.14:** the knife along the forearm on
  waking; the knife held through a question and put back after the answer ("He left the knife where it was."); "The moon's down by
  midnight,"; "She'll dig it crooked."; the cloak over the ear.

#### AUGUST FENN / "AUGURINUS" — the vanished treasure hunter; b. 1934 Philadelphia; crossed 1968, landed AD 41; Puteoli astrologer (documents Ch.2; in person Ch.25–35; dies winter 78–79)
- **Attention / worldview:** systems and patterns: tides, tremors, star tables, the doors' timing, box
  scores. **He sees people last.** His worldview: his real life is the one he is going back to. He has
  never furnished a room here.
- **Vocabulary band:** **in English, frozen in 1968** (see the pool below), with Navy and treasure-hunter
  bravado and Philadelphia vowels. **In Greek, perfect, formal and courtly** after more than three decades. He
  is the only character whose second language is better than his first is current. His English documents
  move from boyish capitals (1967) to small, tired script (1968).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** **excited:** runaway sentences chained on "and," changing subject mid-stream and
  correcting himself ("No, don't tell me"). **Tired:** four-word fragments. **No enumerating, numbering or
  listing habit, ever** (struck by the no-tics law). His pattern-mind shows in *what he talks about*,
  never in a verbal device.
- **Rhythm:** bursts and collapses; asks questions and refuses the answers.
- **Evasion — never says:** his sister's name in person until Ch.35 (his 1968 notebook names Margaret
  freely; the silence grew over the decades). In Ch.25 "estate sale" tells him she is dead, and he does
  not ask whose; he refuses the notebook until Ch.35. That he is afraid of dying here; the word "home" for
  anywhere in this century. He talks about going back in a confident future tense until the day he dies.
  No deathbed conversion and no wisdom.
- **Physicality / silence:** ink-stained fingers, a tremor in the left hand, a 1968 Zippo turned over and
  over that he refills with lamp oil and cannot make work (it catches once, Ch.35). He pats a crying
  woman very fast. A potted basil.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by vocabulary band (frozen-1968 English against perfect Greek) and
  attention (systems before people)
- **The 1968 idiom pool** (each item **at most twice book-wide** in his speech; draw widely, never lean
  on one): holy cow · for crying out loud · beats me · no dice · aw, nuts · swell · the whole shebang ·
  big shot · on the level · you bet · knock it off · scram · whaddaya know · cripes · I'll be a monkey's
  uncle · *Jesus H. Christ* (once only, Ch.25). Period references, each **at most twice**: Chico Ruiz
  stealing home on 21 September 1964, Gene Mauch, Johnny Callison, Jim Bunning's perfect game, Connie
  Mack Stadium, Horn & Hardart on Chestnut Street, Wanamaker's, *American Bandstand*, Ed Sullivan, LBJ,
  Nixon running, Sputnik.
- **Bleed guard:** Mara must never pick up his idiom, and he must never pick up hers. When he hears a
  1990s word he rages at it; he does not adopt it.
- **Chaos on the page (subjects, never phrases):** **the 1964 Phillies** (Ch.25, 26, 29, 35: the 6½-game
  lead with 12 to play). **The automat** (Ch.25 coconut cream; Ch.27 lemon meringue; Ch.35 he can't
  remember the pie). **Sunk cost** (he finishes every terrible jar of wine he has paid for). **The rage**
  (Ch.26 the "voicemail" letter, once only; never repeated in person, so it cannot become a bit).
- **What he knows (fixes the Ch.25 delivery):** he knows from school that Pompeii dies in 79, and he
  rebuilt his door-tables around it after his 71 and 73 dates failed. He believes the doors are one door
  with many mouths, which is what his "it" means in "the one it brought."
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only voice that runs on 1968 American slang in breathless "and"-chains
  and talks about the future as a place he is going, against Mara's precise 1998 English, which will not
  name the future at all.
- **Ch.2 documents as written (Writer, 2026-10-18):** Thanksgiving 1967 entry (Margaret thinks a scholiast is
  a foot doctor; Father Kostas at St. George's translated the scholion for a roast pork sandwich at the
  Terminal; he slit the uncut pages of an 1838 Leipzig edition at the university library with his thumb,
  up to the note and no further; Margaret made him add the question mark after BIBLIOTHECA "to be
  honest"; "two thousand bucks and a better flashlight"). February 1968: sold the Plymouth to a mailman in
  Jenkintown; TWA to Athens 2 April; Margaret tells the church ladies he is in Mexico. Delphi (room over a
  taverna, a goat at the window); the cold-air entry; "Slept up there. Didn't help."; June "Tired
  today."; last entry dated July 21, 1968 (going in "tomorrow first thing", back before Labor Day).
  Consulate carbon dated 16 Sept 1968: one gray wool overcoat with contents of pockets, forwarded to Miss
  Margaret Fenn. No enumerating anywhere; the handwriting change is carried by Mara's description.
- **Sample line:** "You know what kills me? I had two seats for the Labor Day doubleheader, and some guy
  at Connie Mack sat next to an empty chair all afternoon and never knew it was me, and that was, what,
  a hundred years ago, two thousand, for crying out loud."

---

## MAJOR SECONDARIES

#### KALLIA — widow who keeps a pilgrims' lodging by the Castalian spring, Delphi (Ch.5–16; farewell Ch.16)
- **Attention / worldview:** **costs in labor:** who carries the water, who will clean it, how many
  hands a thing takes. The world is chores and the people who do them.
- **Vocabulary band:** Phocian village Greek; household nouns (jar, broom, wool, hearth, beans, pilgrims,
  rent). Nothing learned and nothing quoted (**no proverbs**: struck).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** short imperatives. **She answers a question with a task** (CAPPED from Ch.10 at **1 chore-reply
  per chapter**, and sending someone off on an errand instead of answering counts as the same device; her other replies
  are silence, an act, a question back or a flat fact: see at-risk after Ch.10 FINAL). Her own questions
  are practical (can you carry water, can you card wool, is she Greek, can she weave), and under them sits
  the question she cannot ask.
- **Rhythm:** few words, then work. She never finishes a speech; she goes and does something instead.
- **Evasion — never says:** her son's name, until the letter forces it once (Ch.10); anything about
  herself or her widowhood; thanks; goodbye (Ch.16 is a sweep and a put-away broom, no speech).
- **Physicality / silence:** **never sits** while anyone else is working. Always something in her hands.
  She touches objects, not people (contrast Grata). The broom laid across the empty room's threshold.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (labor and its cost) and evasion (never speaks of
  herself)
- **Bleed guard:** never notices how a thing is *made* (Mara's); only who has to *do* it.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the woman who replies to every question with a job, against Theano, who
  replies to every question with a complaint about herself.
- **Sample line:** "Water first. Cry after. The jar's by the door and the spring won't come to you."
- **Ch.5 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; first live appearance):** about fifty; broad brown face lined
  across the forehead; gray hair dragged back under a dark kerchief. Comes to the Castalian spring after
  dark with a jar on her head and a lamp in her hand, to fill the jar (the page says only "a lamp", no
  material; it gives no reason beyond the errand).
  Her lodging is a short walk above the spring, up the road and along a lane ("near" the spring, not
  beside the basin). **Her own life before
  she sees Mara:** fishes a pilgrims' limp garland out of the basin and flings it down the slope
  ("Boeotians"), then washes each foot under the overflow with her eyes shut. Lines are imperatives and
  practical questions only: "Out of the basin. People drink that." / "Who beat you?" / "Hands." / "Turn
  them." / "Open your mouth." / "What's that? Lead?" (Mara's amalgam filling) / "Who saw you come down
  here?" / "Can you carry water? … card wool? Weave? Grind? Cook?" / "Whose are you?" / "Bring the jar."
  She **never touches Mara**: she inspects hands and teeth by lamplight and demonstrates opening her own
  mouth and carrying on the head. Answers Mara's long English outburst by pointing at the jar (a task).
  The one sentence about her household, not herself: "I've a houseful of Thebans, and the girl ran off
  at the vintage." On the way home she bangs up a neighbor at night to reclaim her fine horsehair sieve
  (lent "a month" ago for the neighbor's wife to strain her must; it comes back with a hole; she threatens to shame the wife at the spring).
  Mara answers her in English throughout, or by a head-shake (the Greek refusal holds from the
  guide's mimicry on); Kallia reads tone, not words ("Mine." is said in English). Her name reaches Mara only when a pilgrim shouts it from the gallery; to "Who's that you've got?" she
  says "A pair of hands" and, to "Can she sing?", "She can carry." Work orders: ashes out before light,
  hearth swept, two jars from the spring before the pilgrims wake, then the floors and the Thebans' pots.
  "Don't touch the oil." Takes the lamp away (lamp oil is lamp oil); later throws a goat-hair blanket in
  from the dark ("Blanket"). **She never asks Mara's name.** No thanks, no sitting, no proverb, no
  speech about herself. Her son's room is NOT shown (held for Ch.7).
- **Ch.6 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; backfill, the afternoon and night before the climb):** buys a string of
  split oak from Valens in the lane. Her own life, not Mara's: stands with her back to the pomegranate trunk, arms
  crooked out, to keep the gray mule out of her court ("She had that tree to the height of her head last winter");
  smells the cut end of a split ("Wet." / "It'll spit. The last lot spat a coal on a Theban's cloak, and I paid for
  the cloak." / "Then it'll spit oak."); haggles ("Four now, and the fifth when I've seen it burn without
  spitting"); pays from a knot in her kerchief corner, then goes at him in Phocian too fast for Mara to follow
  (he answers a word at a time) until he gives her one coin back, and ties the knot up again more slowly than she
  untied it. (Synced to the final page 2026-10-19; every other quote in this block is on the page.) Asks Valens whether the
  sea is shut and whether anything came in from the east before the last ship left Kirrha, then "Letters." /
  "Nobody asked what you carry." (her son; unexplained on the page). Of Mara: "Don't wait for an answer. She
  doesn't talk." Orders: "You. Under the stair. Bark up." To a Theban on the gallery: "When the hearth comes."
  That night, from the dark at the top of the outside stair as Mara leaves with the duffel and her ash shovel:
  "The shovel comes back." Mara thanks her for the blanket in English; Kallia: "Shut the gate behind you." Still
  never touches Mara, never asks her name, never speaks of herself.
- **Ch.7 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; winter AD 70-71. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish,
  disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and the three finalize fixes; every quote below is on the page, checked
  by script):** takes the shovel back ("Let the god put an edge
  back on it.") and says nothing more about the mountain that morning. Dresses Mara in the runaway girl's tunic, cord
  and himation ("She took herself off when the grapes came in, in what she stood up in." / "Not to carry water in. The
  hem hides the boots if you walk right. You don't walk right.", one unbroken speech since cycle 1, with no exit inside
  it). Gives her lessons in the court in the slack hour, ignoring the Thebans' advice; after the cheese-man she "stood
  square before me with a pot in her hands" for the eye lesson.
  Lessons as tasks: walking ("Shorter. From the knee. You're not going up a mountain." / "So the town says." / "You see
  their feet." / "You've no ears. I answer."), SITS once to demonstrate (the only time on the page; up before the stool
  stops rocking: "You sit like you paid for the bench."), eyes, given as ONE speech with no beat inside it ("He was
  talking to me. Look at a man out there and you've asked him something. And somebody answers." / "In here you look at
  the pots."). At the bath she washes standing and lies flatly for Mara ("Singe your own," to the baker's wife; "They
  keep them like that where she's from." / "Over the sea." / "Pox," … "When she was small." / "It took one on her." /
  "Your bread's burning, or your husband's mother is eating it."). Corrects Mara's Greek by saying the word back the
  town's way, with no explanation ("Because it's what they're called."); there is no hand-off in it. Her only questions
  about Mara's world are labor: "Where you come from," … "who carries the water?" / "And the wool?" / "What do the women
  do all day?" / "Who pays for a pot that's broken?" / "There's no such country." / "Then why aren't you in it?" (her
  one "Then" retort in Ch.7) / "I want that basket done before the lamp goes." Of the spring's talk: "Let them. Simple
  girls don't get asked anything. Tighter than that; you're making a bird's nest." Asked who slept in the shut room
  upstairs: "It's swept," then "Tighter, I said" (her deflection is the labor she does on the room). The swept door:
  every morning before her own threshold, the broom left lying across it, the rest of the house done "with the other
  one"; she puts it back without a word when Mara borrows it. With Valens: withholds the fifth coin ("It spat." /
  "Twice. Once on my good hearthstone and once in the lentils." / "keep the gray's teeth off my tree"); "She's simple."
  The broken jar: she looks past the pieces at Mara's wrist; "Who?" / "Did he touch you?" (Mara: "Only my wrist.") /
  "That jar came up from Kirrha on my own head, eleven summers ago, and I've not had to buy one since. Go back down and
  fetch the water in the old one. It leaks." Then she sweeps the pieces into the ash pail with her toe (the section no
  longer closes on her water order). That night: the Syracuse plan ("You're my husband's brother's girl." / "From
  Syracuse." / "West, by water. Past Corinth, past everywhere." / "Your people died, and you came on one of the last
  boats before the sea shut, after the vintage."). Mara asks "What was he called? Your husband's brother." and Kallia
  talks straight past it ("They talk strange over there; nobody here has heard enough of it to say how strange. If he
  asks you anything, you've a cold."); the question is never answered. Then "The whole spring knows you came on a
  boat. Bank the hearth before you lie down." At the council: "Free." / "My husband's brother's girl. From Syracuse."
  / "She came on one of the last boats, after the vintage." / "Half Syracuse is Roman," / "I do." / "Who stands for my
  house, if I don't? You've had no other name on that line for years. Write me." She has NEVER asked Mara's name and
  has none to give the clerk; she hears it there, standing beside Mara. At home: "What did he put?" / "Say your name
  for me." / "Maira. The water." (said the Delphian way, nearer *Mera*: the first time she uses a name for Mara). Last
  scene: "At midwinter the women climb Parnassus." / "They go every other winter, and this is the winter. You'll come.
  You've the boots for it." / "Bind your feet in rags over the boots, and bring the girl's himation." / to "And what is
  it for?": "Dance. Drink. Freeze. Not as high as your cave." (one line; the beat that split it was cut). Still never
  touches Mara; still never speaks of herself (the jar and the house only).

- **Ch.8 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; the night on Parnassus. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after
  dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair and polish cycle 1; every quote below is on the page, checked by
  script):** opens mid-instruction, down on her heels at the hearthstone binding her own shoe in blanket strips, "her
  hands did it while her eyes were on the pot": "And once we're above the last house you say nothing about men, not
  husbands and not sons, whatever they've done to you this winter," … "Up there you talk about your feet, or the cold, or
  you hold your tongue. Wind it harder than that, or it'll be round your ankle before you're halfway up the stair." ("not
  sons" is hers to say and she does not explain it; the order-or-you'll-fall-before-a-place threat is this one, hers,
  once.) To "Why nothing about men?": "Because it's the mountain. Tear another; there's more blanket than there is
  evening." To "And if they ask me things?": "They'll ask you everything, every step up and every step down." / "You tell
  them what I told the clerk, and not a word over." (Ch.7: Mara gave the clerk only her name; Kallia supplied the rest.)
  To the Thebans' heckle: "The month's due on the day, wine or none," … "If the house burns down, you'll pay for the
  house." (her money axis; the lentil reply is gone). To "Where on the plain do they dance?": "Where they danced the last
  time." and then she moves the pin, "two fingers on the bronze and none on me" (no line with it: the draft's hitch-up order was cut;
  still never touches Mara). In the lane: "Nikaso," … "you've a sieve of mine to mend before you borrow anything else in
  this lane." / "When she likes." / "Do it at the top, where it's flat," … "I'll not lay you out on a stair." Her one
  "Then" retort: "Then you should have borrowed her boots." **Carrying is hers:** she climbs with the wineskin slung
  across her back on a cord ("Kallia carries it and I hold it", Nikaso), and her labor axis on the rite is "Somebody has
  to carry him, as somebody hauls the water." (the eval flags it as a near-proverb, #16; her card strikes proverbs, so no
  second such line). **Dances** with her kerchief off and her gray hair down to her shoulder blades ("I had seen it only
  once before, plastered flat at the bath"), "stiff-elbowed, with her chin up, and she danced as she scoured a pot, all of
  her at once, with no expression on her face" (the rarity gloss is spent on this: her hair; the second gloss, on the hold of her himation, was cut,
  so rarity glosses are at 1). After the cave she looks at "the knees of the himation, which were black with wet" (the
  knees only; the pitch is gone) and goes "to help Philista get the women into a line" (her one walk-off, no "Then" in
  it). On the way: "Your knees are wet through," / "In there." / "Did you find what you went for?" (her one question
  about the cave; Mara: "No. It was shut."). **The Oracle (opens toward Ch.9):** "there's a god in the town who answers
  questions. He doesn't answer them in winter. Once the road's clear of snow he sits down in his house and answers
  whoever's paid." / "Women can't go in to him. A man goes in for you, with your question in his mouth, and brings you out
  the answer." / "Whoever you can get." / "It costs." / "More than you've got. Ask a priest. He'll tell you more than it
  is." **Her varied reply (per the Ch.7 note):** to "Have you asked him anything? The god?" she answers with a question
  back, "Who would I send in?", and no chore follows: "She walked on, and the wind took her flame flat and let it go
  again." To Mara's question about the god in the basket: "He rides down on her head in the morning, and they put him
  back in his house till the next time." **In the pen she crouches, never sits** ("Kallia crouched beside me with her shoes
  off": her ONE posture beat in the chapter since the 2026-10-19 kneel/crouch trim; later she simply "let it go by her
  like weather"), her rags turned to the fire "like flatbreads on a
  griddle"; says nothing through the farce, then fishes a sandal out of the fire with a burning fennel stalk, flicks it
  clear and feeds the stalk to the fire ("And who's going down that stair barefoot in the morning? Who's to carry her?");
  backs Mara ("Give it her, and hush," … "She'll do it if you hush."); "Kallia was no help" over the missing husband (she
  "did not add one now"); Sostrata's eye goes past her without a verse, "which suited Kallia"; at Mara's joke she "bent
  her face over her rags and did not raise it". **Lies down** at the outer edge of the heap, on her side, wind at her back,
  dried rags under her cheek: "Wake me when it's light," … "Those Thebans will have the pot scraped dry by now, and burnt
  the scrapings." Never speaks of herself.

- **Ch.9 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; one short scene at home. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after
  dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair and polish cycle 1; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):**
  "Kallia was in the court at home, scouring the big pot with sand." No look at Mara's hand first (continuity W-06 cut it as the
  Ch.7 broken-jar template). Her only question is "Into your hand?" (cycle 1 cut "Who paid?" / "Euthydikos." as the Ch.7
  hearthstone skeleton). To "Onto it. From above." **her varied reply is an act, not a chore and not a question back:** "To
  that she gave no word at all. She filled the basin at the jar and stood it on the hearthstone beside the coins. A lump of the
  gray washing clay went down next to it." Her one chore beat: "The sand hissed round the belly of the pot." To the god's price
  (one line from Mara, no list): "Who takes the goat up?" / "Then you'll want a man a goat will walk for. They don't, for most."
  (her one "Then" retort; her labor axis). Close: "Kallia's sand had gone quiet while I tied it." (she stops work to watch the
  rag knotted; no turn-away, no walk-off). Never sits, never touches Mara, never speaks of herself. No broom, no shut room, no
  letter (Ch.10's). **Ch.10 is hers:** no gaze-arrival "then" for her (eval Cross-Book #4), no hearthstone exchange in the Ch.7/9
  shape, no body rebel through Mara's hands.

- **Ch.10 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; one afternoon at home. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish,
  hook-craft, the disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and the PATH fix; every quote below is on the page, checked by
  script):** takes the letter at the gate ("Kallia had it out of his fingers and held it shut in her hand.") and asks only
  "Where from?"; to the Elean's "He said it might be bad": "Her face gave him nothing for that." To the Elean, money and labor:
  "Water," (to Mara: the promised cup, paid cheaply) / "That was a cup." / "Then drink in Elis." (her one "Then" retort) /
  "Full." / "The house is full." / "There's the houses by the stadium." **Carries the letter unread in her belt from noon to
  mid-afternoon** ("She pushed the letter down behind her belt, against her stomach, and returned to the pots."; "The corner of
  it showed over her belt when she reached up for the oil."; "Once, going past the jar, she touched the place through her
  clothes."); gives the man from Patrae his bread "twice over" (a slip, unremarked). **Stands to spin** "beside the lean-to that
  held the hearth and all the kitchen the house had", and first, as plain seasonal work on "the first afternoon of the year with
  heat enough in the sun to work without a cloak", lets down the lean-to's summer blind (moved here by polish cycle 1 so that it
  answers nothing): a reed blind "rolled up under the eave"; she "pulled the slip-knot on its cord one-handed. The blind dropped
  open to the ground between the jar and the sun, letting down a winter's soot and a dead wasp. She came back to her place."
  "Presently she let it hang." (the spindle; a hand freed for the letter). Holds the letter toward Mara "across the wool at the
  length of her arm": "You read the clerk's wax," (her only acknowledgment that Mara reads). Her replies during the reading:
  "There's gods enough here," / "Which arm?" / "He was never any use with the left," / "Two and a half," … "At the vintage." /
  "Is she Greek?" / "Can she weave?" / "Anybody keeps a house." … "Go on." / "Here it's for the father's father." (left
  unanswered) / **the yawn** (involuntary, face and eyes, twice; Mara takes it for tiredness) / "A roof you sit on," … "Where does
  the rain go?" / "They'll have enough." / "The man," … "Bought?" / "The middle of winter's gone by." / "It's come, then,
  whichever it is." / answering the greetings to the letter itself ("She was answering the letter"): "She's living," …
  "Thrasyllos died when the olives were in, the year after. *Let me take*, he says. He took them, at ten years old, and told
  nobody, and Thrasyllos walked to Kirrha after him in his bare feet. Glauke's girls are married. The big one's in Amphissa with a
  potter, treading his clay for him." (her one memory of him, given as neighbor news). At the letter's end, stillness: "Kallia
  stood in the sun with the wool in her hand." To Mara's "He says the arm has knit," no word: "She drew a fresh pull of wool from
  the hank, and let it run back." / "Which is his name?" ("She looked at it from where she stood, and came no nearer.") / "Keep
  it out of the smoke," / to Nikaso, flat: "He's living." / "He sends you greeting. You and your husband." / "He's married, in
  Syria, and there's a child." / "That Elean was promised a cup of wine for carrying it, and got water." (no errand) / to "How
  old was he, when he went?": a task, "Put a plate on those, or the mice will be in." (her one chore-reply in the chapter) / to
  Mara's offer to write back: "On what?" / then a question nobody asked (the disruptor's interruption, left unexplained): "The
  horse-seller," … "His name again." (Mara: "Iamblichos.") / "And whoever carries it to Syria?" / "Tell him," (unfinished; she
  "wound the yarn up, all of it") / at the foot of the stair: "Tell him the baker's old mother is living." / an obol from her belt
  "on the bottom stair, where I would have to pass it." … "For the sheet." **The broom (anchor):** "Kallia lifted the broom off
  its boards and swept them. At dawn they had been swept already; I had been carrying out the ashes when she did them." "She
  took it to the lip of the gallery in short strokes, and nothing came over, and back to the door. Once more she went over them,
  along the grain", then "laid the broom over the threshold again, the head against one post and the handle against the other,
  and came down." Her good himation: "She covered her head with it, kerchief and all, and closed it under her chin." **Says his
  name once**: "That Elean will have been all round the stadium by now," … "and the houses there are full to the tiles. He can
  have Ariston's room, at the three obols. If he comes to the gate before I find him, he waits in the court." Exit by the gate
  only ("The gate came to behind her and bounced once."; no gait or footwear), and "Up at the last door the broom still lay over
  the threshold, from post to post." There is no exchange about opening the room. **Never sits, never touches Mara, no
  gaze-arrival "then", no hearthstone shape, no bending, no kerchief-knot payment, never speaks of herself.** Leaves the letter
  with Mara (it goes in the niche "where Kallia kept the good knife and a twist of cumin"). Page counts: chore-replies 1;
  errands 0; name-asks 2; work-suspended tell 2 expressive uses (the wool in her hand; the pull run back) plus the functional
  "Presently she let it hang."; "living" ×3 in her mouth (×5 in the chapter) and "cup" ×6, both CLOSED after Ch.10.

  its circuit of the pot." / "There's women in this lane twenty years married who'd not do that much for a man for nothing." (the

  herself." She has NOT been shown the notice or connected it to Valens (keep her from it). Her obol for the papyrus is still unspent.

#### THEANO — the Pythia; a farmer's widow off duty (glimpsed Ch.8 unnamed, shouting at crows; Ch.13)
- **Attention / worldview:** her own body (her knees, the descent into the adyton), the priests' petty
  politics, the crows. She treats the god like a difficult employer and the oracle like a job with bad
  conditions.
- **Vocabulary band:** plain mountain Phocian Greek, a farmer's widow's words. **No oracular verse, no
  riddles, no portent**, except when she repeats the god's public answer, which is *his* language and not
  hers.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** grumbling run-on complaint, then a flat fact delivered with no softening.
- **Fact:** she goes down into the adyton on the monthly consultation days of the warm season. In Ch.13
  she takes Mara's last two ibuprofen for her knees without thanks. She
  refuses questions or answers them in one word.
- **Rhythm:** a long complaint, then a short answer, then a return to the complaint.
- **Evasion — never says:** anything comforting; any explanation of the god, the answer or the hour (she
  explains herself once, about the crows, Ch.13: "Because they come." … "A thing that'll sit quiet at a
  burying can have my barley."; continuity W-13 / ENTITY CH13-13, card amended 2026-10-20); any hint of
  the hour. Whether the hour is "not hers to give" or "not given," she will not say which.
- **Physicality / silence:** sits whenever she can. Her knees are what she talks about, not a gesture she
  repeats: one knee beat per appearance (Ch.8 "She kept her free hand on her knee the whole time"; Ch.13
  "She pressed her knees with the heels of her hands."); a return gets a new beat or none. Feeds the
  crows the god's barley, shouts at them for taking it, and lies about it.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (her body and her grievances) and evasion (no
  comfort; no explanation of the god or the hour)
- **Bleed guard:** must not sound like Kallia (see the guarantee) or like a fantasy oracle.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only character who talks freely about herself and gives nothing away,
  against Kallia, who gives a stranger a roof and never says a word about herself.
- **Sample line:** "Once a month all summer that god has me down a hole on these knees, and those men won't
  spare me a stool. Well? Ask it. It's going to rain."

- **Ch.8 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; UNNAMED, unrecognised, texture only. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19 after
  continuity W-05/W-15):** an old woman on a block of stone by her door at the last gate but one, a basket of barley in
  her lap, throwing it "over the frozen ruts a fistful at a time" to the crows ("black on the white rime, walking about
  with their shoulders up") and shouting at them for taking it: "Black thieves," … "every one of you. That's not for
  you." / "Go on, then. Take it. Choke on it." "She kept her free hand on her knee the whole time, and her eyes on the
  crows." (The draft's clause about whose barley it was and the no-look sentences were cut: no wink.) Nobody names her or explains her.
  Ch.13 recognises her by situation only (the crows); do not re-quote these lines there.
- **Ch.13 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; FIRST LIVE APPEARANCE AND NAMED. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ3;
  every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** **Seen unrecognized at dawn:** "old, and veiled to the eyes in white wool, with a
  sprig of laurel in her hand", led up the ramp by two priests "at the pace of her slowest foot", halting on every temple step "while she
  brought her second foot up beside the first" (a walk-on: "That's her," … "She'll be going down now."). **Named by the jug boy** ("Theano
  wants her.") and by Kallia ("The god's woman," … "She'll have you carry her water up while you're there. She does that." Theano does not).
  **Recognized by situation only (CH8-2):** "the house next to the highest", a squared stone at the door "gone round at the corners with
  being sat on", "a dozen crows waited in a row", parched barley flung "over the ruts"; no Ch.8 line re-quoted (her new scolding: "Look at
  that," … "Robbers. The minute a woman sits down." / "Get off, you, you've had yours."). **One knee beat:** "She pressed her knees with the
  heels of her hands." **Body:** "a farmer's hands, square, the nails broken short, and a face the sun had burned brown and crumpled under
  the eyes"; bare feet "broad, cracked at the heels, feet that had walked behind a plow". **Mara's city, flat:** "Syracusan," said Theano,
  "as flat as a price for a lame ewe, and left it at that." / "Sit down; my neck won't go back that far. Not there. That's the cat's." **Her
  grievances (she talks freely about herself):** the stool ("not a man of them will carry a stool down after me. They've stools; they've a
  stool for the square beard" … "They said they'd put it to the council. The council! For a stool!"); her husband who "dragged this stone
  here for me before he died" … "with two oxen and a lever and a deal of cursing"; "Listen to them. You can hear them from there, can't
  you?" (Mara cannot); "Down," … "Up, I can lean on the wall."; "Fourteen years I've had that god, since my husband went" (Pythia after
  widowhood, over fifty at selection); "When I started there were three of us to take turns" … "Now there's only me"; her sister's son keeps
  the ewes and the cheese money; the god as an employer ("he wants you there before it's light, and he's never satisfied with the goat, and
  he takes the credit"). **Chaos (the lie):** "A laurel leaf was burned into the rim of the basket, small and brown." (no pointer back to
  the altar: the cake stall's trays carry the same leaf "burned into the wood of the rim", and the link is the reader's); "That's my own
  barley," … "Off my own field, below the stadium. I wouldn't give them a grain of his." / "The brand on the basket was by her foot. I left
  it there." **Her one explanation, about the crows only (CH13-13; audit W-13; card amended):** "Why give them any?" / "Because they come."
  … "They were up on that ridge the day my husband went out of this door feet first, the whole row of them, and not a crow of them made a
  sound, which the mourners never managed. A thing that'll sit quiet at a burying can have my barley." She explains nothing of the god, the
  answer or the hour. **The ibuprofen (one beat):** "What's that?" / she "took them off my palm with two fingers", "put both pills in her
  mouth at once, drank from the cup and swallowed, wiped her mouth on the back of her wrist", no thanks, another fistful at the crows; never
  mentioned again. **She heard the carrier from below:** "shifting about over your head like a mule in a stall, scraping, changing feet" …
  "Yours stood. I'd have said nobody was there, except the floor gave when he stepped to it." / "The rest of them, I can tell you what they
  ate before they came." **The priests' dressing of the answer:** "They put a mountain in it," … "And a sea, and a painted tomb, and
  something to tap your foot to." … "whatever I bring up from down there, they take it and put shoes on it. He said it short and he said it
  plain. Nobody pays to travel down from Thessaly for short and plain." / of the non-Greek word: "Not Greek, so not the god's, says the
  square beard. The smoke talking, or the woman down the hole. The god's been everywhere; he gets about more than they do." / to "before
  they dressed it": "That's not what you climbed my street for." **Never comforts:** why she sent: "Because my knees hurt," … "and I wanted
  to tell somebody who hadn't heard it. Ask." **The WHEN withheld (canon):** "That's not mine to give you." … "Or it was never given me.
  Which of those it is, is mine to know." / to silver: "Keep your silver. Whatever's paid on this hill goes to the priests, and I get the
  steps." / to "Was there a day in it?": "You've had your answer to that," (said "watching the crows") "Ask the other." **The WHERE, first
  source (canon):** to "Which city?", the chapter's last line: "Theano shifted on the stone, and grunted with it." / "Pompeii." (no hand on
  a knee). Her house: one swept room, a loom with a brown-and-white striped blanket half woven, a cat asleep "in the warm ash with its paws
  tucked under it" ("Nothing in it was the god's."). **Spent in Ch.13:** the stool-and-council complaint as a set piece; the husband's
  stone; the crows at the burying (her one explanation); hearing the carrier's feet; "put shoes on it"; "Ask the other."; the branded
  basket. If she returns: new grievances, never these, and no further explanation.


- **Ch.14 (no live lines; Writer, 2026-10-20. SYNCED 2026-10-20: FINAL page, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ7; quotes checked by script):** after
  "Pompeii." (Ch.13's last word) "She had nothing more for me. She had me carry her water instead." (Kallia's Ch.13 prediction, not pointed
  at): "Inside her door, beside the loom, her big jar stood empty, with a small one turned upside down on its mouth." (FZ4); the fountain
  "where the street turned below her house, a lion's head in a wall with moss under its lip"; when the jar comes back "She was indoors
  already." and "she said nothing of it at all"; "I tipped it into her big jar; the cat opened one eye at me." Earlier, on the doorstep:
  "Theano told it to get off, and it stayed, and she let it." (a crow; the barley already gone). Explains nothing. To Kallia her summons is
  reported as the stool.

#### FELIX — Valens's mule-boy, bought off a Kirrha slaver c. AD 70 aged ~12 and freed with no papers; later sworn to Pompeii's gladiator school (Ch.11–40)
- **Attention / worldview:** fame, fights, who is winning, who is watching. The arena is the most glorious
  thing in the world, and he wants exactly the life that nearly destroyed Valens.
- **Vocabulary band:** fast, boastful street Greek (Part One) that turns into Pompeian Latin slang (Part
  Two); fighters' names and arena terms (thraex, murmillo, retiarius, the palm, the wooden sword). He
  grows from boy to young man (about 21 at the eruption) and the slang matures with him.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** **stacks two or three questions before anyone answers one.** Boasts in
  superlatives. Interrupts himself with a new question.
- **Rhythm:** fast, overlapping, never a pause.
- **Evasion — never says:** that he is afraid of Valens's disapproval; that he is afraid at all; "thank
  you" to Valens.
- **Physicality / silence:** always moving: bouncing on his heels, climbing, leaning. Scratches a tiny palm
  branch on walls he leans against (an action, never a phrase; on the page at most three times: Ch.21,
  28, and one more if earned). Sleeps anywhere, including against a mule.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by rhythm (stacked questions) and attention (fame and fights)
- **Bleed guard:** no catchphrase and no boast formula repeated; vary the boast every time.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the one who asks three things at once and boasts about all of them, against
  Lucius, who asks one thing gravely and waits for the answer.
- **Sample line:** "Did you see the net? Did you see how he turned it? Is it true you fought at Corinth
  with two cracked fingers and won anyway? Why do I have to hear that from a lanista?"

- **Ch.11 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; first live appearance. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20, after dialogue-polish (every
  burst re-cut for opener variety), hook-craft, the disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and finalize line F3; every quote
  below is on the page, checked by script):** **Entry (all four marks):** a voice first ("a boy was telling some animal at the top of
  his voice that it was the worst mule between Parnassus and the sea"); the physical anchor: "The boy had a shovel taller than he
  was. He was a thin one of twelve or so, with straw in his hair." (polish cycle 1 cut the tunic-belt-hair skeleton, the Ch.4
  goat-boy's introduction re-nouned; the knife-cut hair was cut earlier); behavior: "He had me in his eye before I was well through,
  and came straight at me with the shovel."; tension: he wants Valens's approval and will not say so ("Why won't he let me? I know
  poultices. Did he say anything to you about me doing it?"). **Stacked questions, openers rotated (highest two-word frame 2):** "Are
  you Kallia's? You're the one he took up to Pan's in the snow. Did you hit Pan? Which hand? Was there much blood?" (and off "still
  asking, as he went, which hand") / "What's he got in his pouch that ticks? Yours, is it? Each day before he's had his bread he does
  something to it, with his back to me, and if I ask he sends me for water. Does it live? He won't let me near it, and I held a
  fowler's hawk down at the harbor, bare-armed, and it never put a claw in me." / "He doesn't keep things for people," / "Where'd you
  learn leather? Off your father? Had he mules? There was a sailor told me in Syracuse they eat octopus raw. Raw!" / "Chian are
  bigger," / "I never had a Caunian till this winter," / **his own problem, seeded (arena-mad):** "There's boxing at the gymnasium
  before the god sits. A Thessalian, and a man from Elateia who killed somebody at Thebes, they say, with his fists, with one blow.
  Everybody's going. Kallia's lodgers are going. Can I go? I'll have the stalls done before noon." / "It costs you nothing. I'll walk
  there and back, I'll be here before the string is. I'd go a round with the Thessalian myself, if they'd let me in the ring. Have you
  ever seen a man killed with one blow?" / "You've never seen anything." (F3: no tag, no "with great bitterness"; "Felix took himself
  off to fork out the stalls, and forked them so hard that the dog left the yard.") / at the hoof: "Do you want the twitch?" … "I'll
  get the twitch. I'll hold her ear; nobody holds an ear better than me. Or the rope on her hind leg?" / "That's the bad in her,
  coming out?" … "All of it? She'll go to Kirrha tomorrow, won't she?" / the poultice morning: "He was up all night with her. There's
  three sacks gone, and I never heard him once." / to Praxias's quail (the fights axis): "That's the one? From behind the stadium,
  that blinded the Amphissan's bird in one eye? The left, I heard. Did it fight again after, the Amphissan's?" / the report (speed;
  the chapter's one cut-off and its one em-dash): "He went to the wine-seller under the plane tree … and the baker said—" / "The
  square? There's no quails in the square." … "The baker feeds his bird on wine, everybody says so. Wine, to make it brave. Would it
  work on a mule?" / the barred gate: "Who's coming? The Amphissan, about his bird? We've never had it barred in the day, not since I
  came. Is it you?" / "The Amphissan, though?" / from the top of the gate: "Nobody. A cart of oil. Some Thessalians, lost. Nobody. I
  can see clear to the bend from up here, and past it if I stand." / his name: "What's that, writing? A curse? Who's it on? Can you
  write me? Big?" / "Valens! She's written me! Come and see what I look like!" / "Read me," … "Go on, then. Say me out loud." / "He
  can't. Nobody in this yard can but you. Is that one me, with its arms on its hips? Will it wash off, if it rains?" / "Nobody at
  Kirrha's got his name written down. The Cilician called me Boy from Side to Kirrha. Has he told you about the Cilician?" **His
  story, boasted by him (Valens tells none of it):** "He bought me off him on the quay at Kirrha. He paid more for me than he paid for
  the black, and she's four years old and sound, or she was. The Cilician laughed and said nobody had ever paid him so much for a boy
  with a cough. You'd think he hit the Cilician. He never. He just looked at him, and the Cilician gave him a drachma back. You've
  seen how he looks at people." / "The name's his. Felix. It's lucky, in Latin. He talks to the mules in Latin, did you know that?" /
  "He said I could go wherever I liked, and I said where, and he said wherever I liked, and I came here." / "Can I keep it? The dog's
  never seen a name." / to Mara's "Did he take you to the priests," … "and have it cut on the wall?": "What wall?" No paper, no god,
  no wall. His name, ΦΗΛΙΞ, is scratched in capitals on a sherd he keeps. **Four boasts, four shapes (no boast formula):** the
  hawk; the ring; the ear; the bend. **Speed, in his acts and his mouth only:** "Felix did nothing at a walk."; climbs the stable
  rubble to the reeds and drops back down it "though the door was beside him"; eats standing; "he was already on the roof"; "Felix
  was already halfway to the shed"; "Felix had it there, lit."; "Felix left his bucket and went to him."; "The boy was out of the gate
  and after him." (polish cycle 1 removed the three "before [X] had finished" beats; the construction survives once, in the protected
  first sentence). **The anchor:** asleep in the end stall, "with his back to the stall's wall and his head fallen sideways against
  her belly, mouth ajar, and the sack he had been sent to change still in his fist"; the black, who had "kicked his bucket over every
  evening", is "standing square on all four feet, the bad one as well, which she had not done since it went lame, because to take her
  weight off it she would have had to shift, and he was asleep against her." **Knows:** believes Valens cannot read ("He can't. Nobody
  in this yard can but you."); knows the trip up to Pan's and that the watch is Mara's (Mara: "It is mine," … "He is keeping it for
  me."; ENTITY CH11-4); has no word of the notice, the arena or Lupus (Ch.21's lanista is where he hears of Corinth). **Not used, keep
  it so:** the palm-branch scratch (Ch.21, 28, one more if earned); a boast formula used twice; "thank you" to Valens; any
  catchphrase. **WATCH for Ch.12+ (final-page counts):** question openers are rotated (highest two-word frame 2: "Can I…?" 2, "Did
  you/he/it" one each, "Is it you?" 1, "Have you ever…" 1); keep rotating, and give him fewer lines in Ch.12 (he brings the goat home)
  rather than another burst. "already [somewhere]" for his speed: 2, AT CAP. "nobody" in his speech: 6 (WATCH). Exits on a line plus
  a tag and "and" ("He doesn't keep things for people," said Felix, and was gone. / "What wall?" he said, and went out with his
  sherd to show the dog.): 2 after F3, AT CAP; Ch.12 at most 1. The yard dog as his foil: 3 beats; rest it.

- **Ch.12 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; fewer lines than Ch.11, as briefed. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20 (polish cycle 1 +
  F1–F2; continuity W-22): every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** chose the goat at Kirrha ("This one," … "She's got a
  temper. The god likes a temper."); leads her on barley ("held his fist out behind him as he walked"); misses the fight and comes
  running back "with his fist open and nothing in it"; **sees the dead man and is sick** ("He was sick against it, neatly, propped on
  one arm against the stones."), then asks "Who gets his knife?" (unanswered: "Valens came past him to me."). Saw the man Mara hit run
  into the olives and stacks questions at the camera: "What's that? Is it silver? I saw one of them go off through the olives holding
  on to his head. You hit him with it?" (gets "It was my father's,"). Lies about being sick: "It was that crust," … "It was off."
  Invents a hero's version, telling Mara's blow as his own: "I could say I saw them run. Two of them, into the olives, and I chased
  them with a stone, and the tall one had a sword, and I hit him over the head and he dropped it." … "I could have the sword." /
  "That's nothing to tell." **Lies to the graybeard, well:** "We came on him," … "His purse was cut. We came on him and came on up,
  because of the oil." ("His chin was up"); "He believed me," … "He sounded surprised." Carries the goat on his shoulders when she lies
  down ("her forelegs in one fist and her hind legs in the other"); **names her Spicula** ("After the murmillo. The one the emperor
  gave a house to." / "The god won't mind what she's called."); "She's doing it on purpose," … "Is she? Is she ill? Are goats sick when
  they see a dead man?"; asks Mara "Did you see him do it?" / "Quick, though?" and Valens "Did he go down straight off?" (answered
  "No."). The narrator notes his silence once ("he asked nothing whatever, which in my hearing he had never managed before"): one dry
  aside, never a running joke. **Knows now:** Valens killed a man (not the arena, not Lupus: he was ahead with the goat when the names
  were said, and at the ford during the telling); that Mara hit one; that she has a heavy foreign thing that was her father's, which he
  took for silver (a gossip risk for Ch.15); the cover story. **Counts (FINAL):** question openers What / Is ×3 / "You hit…?" / Who /
  Are / Did ×2 / "Quick, though?"; "Was" 0; **"Is" ×3 = WATCH** (rotate in Ch.13); "nobody" 0; exits by line + tag + "and" 0; "already"
  0. Spent: the vomit-then-loot beat; the crust lie; the invented sword; "He believed me,"; Spicula (the name may recur only as an
  object fact in Ch.13, never as a joke).

- **Ch.13 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ3; every quote below is on the page,
  checked by script):** brought the goat from the yard (there "since sundown yesterday") before light; bought her a white wool fillet "with
  an obol of his own"; "He had washed his face for the god and missed his neck." The Elateian boxer: "That's him," … "The one from Elateia,
  with the ears, look at his ears." / "Would he show me his hands, do you think, if I asked him? After?" / later "He let me," … "that
  knuckle killed the man. It's harder than wood." … "He said I'm too thin. He said come back when I've eaten." Watches the sacrifice "in
  silence" (no sickness; the goat's name not used); "Later he was over among the Thessalians by the Elateian, studying his hands." **His
  name in the god's book:** "Me? In the god's book?" … "Where did he put me, under her? Under Praxias? Was it wax he wrote it on, or the
  other, the—" (the chapter's only em-dash) / "What did it look like?" / then sits, "slowly for him", asks nothing more, "His ears had gone
  red to the tips." **His mother, once, unplanned:** "What's it for?" … "That's all? On a god's house? My mother could have told them that."
  / "He stopped, surprised at himself, and scowled at the pomegranate." (never explained; do not build on it without a plan). **Disobeys**
  "The boy does as he's told": "Felix vaulted the wall beside me." (no "at a run"), after a Phocian boy with a wine-stained head bandage;
  dropped the cloak and was back at Mara's side before the rod arrived, "admiring the sky" ("I took him for the one you hit," … "I'd have
  had him."). **Kept the camera secret:** "I never said about your father's silver thing," … "A stone, I told him." / "It wasn't a stone,
  was it?" On the walk down: "He goes like this when a mule dies," / "Not this year. Not that he's told me." (one speech, no pause beat).
  "Didn't the priest say more?" … "You were in there two men's turns." **Arena-mad, Capua:** "They've the schools at Capua in Italy, the big
  ones, a sailor at Kirrha says, with the iron gate" … "If it's Italy, can I come?" (unanswered; sent to water the string; "He went backward
  as far as the gate, and from the gate he ran.": a gait exit by hand, invisible to the gate). Still knows nothing of the arena, Lupus or
  Corinth. Question openers: Would / Me? / In / Where / Under / Was ×1 / What ×2 / That's all? / On / a tag ("was it?") / Didn't / What if /
  can I; "Is" 0; "nobody" 0; line+tag+"and" exits 0; "already" 0.

- **Ch.14 (off-page, Writer, 2026-10-20. SYNCED 2026-10-20: FINAL page, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ7; verbatim, checked by script):** with the
  string at the yard. His Ch.13 "If it's Italy, can I come?" is answered in Mara's plan only: "Felix had asked that afternoon whether he
  could come, if it was Italy. It was Italy. The plan had no boy in it, and I kept it so." (her intention; ARCH-9 untouched; no narrator
  reach.)

#### PLAUTIA — Celer's divorced wife; the woman Valens was framed with (Ch.16–17, Ch.22)
- **Attention / worldview:** what people want and admire, and what can be given; children (her son
  Marcus, b. autumn AD 64: seven in Ch.16, nine in Ch.22; kept by his father under Roman law). She has learned that everything can be taken, so
  she gives it away first.
- **Vocabulary band:** an aristocrat's exquisite Latin (in Corinth also polished Greek); legal terms used
  correctly (dowry, the *actio rei uxoriae*, guardianship).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** formal, subordinate-clause-heavy sentences **broken by sudden blunt
  questions.** **No contractions.**
- **Rhythm:** a long, beautifully built sentence, then a short question like a slap.
- **Evasion — never says:** anything about the night she was framed; Celer's name, if she can avoid it
  (she says "my former husband").
- **Physicality / silence:** gives away anything anyone admires (the bracelet, a cloak, her lamp), then
  grieves for it in the next room. She is very still in public and veiled in court.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (formal periods broken by blunt questions) and evasion
  (the night)
- **Bleed guard:** must not take on Celer's warm "we." Her formality is cool and exact, his is warm and
  slippery.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the aristocrat whose perfect Latin cracks into one blunt question, against
  Celer, whose geniality never cracks at all.
- **Sample line:** "My former husband, who keeps my son in a house on the Craneion with a nurse I have
  never been permitted to meet, has asked the court to consider his reputation; do you think the boy
  still knows my face?"
- **Ch.12 (mention only, in Valens's telling; her first naming on the page):** "He wanted a man to walk behind his wife, one that
  people would know from the sand and stand back from. Plautia." Her walks: "To the baths. To her mother's house. Out to Kenchreai, to
  the Egyptian goddess, on her days." At the frame: "She didn't look at me." After: "Her brother took her home," … "Celer kept the
  boy." Her brother "gone to law for her dowry", and his freedman found Valens at the Kirrha harbor. She does not speak and is not
  described; keep her voice and her body for Ch.16. Her sample line's "a house on the Craneion" stays calibration only (continuity
  W-06); Ch.16 must not use it.

#### QUINTUS ARRIUS CELER — legate on the proconsul's staff at Corinth until Nero's grant of freedom (late 67), out of office at the frame (69), back on the restored proconsul's staff by 71–72; the defendant in Rome (Ch.12 by proxy, Ch.17, Ch.22)
- **Attention / worldview:** standing, reputations, who is watching, which way a room leans. Everyone is
  a friend he has not yet needed. Settled by dowry law, never by violence.
- **Vocabulary band:** senatorial Latin worn lightly; easy, social, unhurried.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** genial **first-person plural** ("we," "us," "all of us"), conditionals and soft
  questions that expect agreement. **Uses contractions** and warmth. Never raises his voice.
- **Rhythm:** patient, even, as if there were all the time in the world.
- **Evasion — never says:** the word *wife*. He says "the lady Plautia." He never makes a direct threat
  and never admits a debt.
- **Physicality / silence:** relaxed, smiling, hand on an arm. **Will not walk under ladders or
  scaffolding,** even in a city being rebuilt; his men carry him around it.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (genial "we," conditionals) and evasion (never "wife,"
  never a threat)
- **Bleed guard:** no villain monologue and no cruelty on the surface.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only speaker who talks to an enemy as an old friend in the first-person
  plural, against Plautia, who addresses everyone in the second person and in earnest.
- **Sample line:** "Sit, please, both of you; nobody here is in any hurry, and I'd much rather we were
  all friends again by the time the wine comes."
- **Ch.12 by proxy (Writer, 2026-10-20; in Valens's account and in the men he sent. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20: every quote
  below is on the page, checked by script):** the man who frames by apology: "He was sorry about it. He said so, more than once, with
  her standing there." "Celer was legate then, on the proconsul's staff."; after the freedom "He'd stayed on at Corinth with nothing to
  be legate of. He had debts. He'd bought a house he couldn't pay for, out where the cypresses are."; the dowry share ("Put a wife away
  for that, and you keep a share of what she brought."); his steward took the tablet; "Celer kept the boy." **His legal claim (W-21;
  see the VALENS card):** not that the freedom went unregistered, but a prior sale: "His story was that he'd bought me from the lanista
  the month before the games, so the lanista had no right to free me, and the magistrate had written down a mistake." In plot terms, a
  backdated bill of sale (not page words). **His men:** a friendly man who was on the benches at Corinth ("I had two drachmas on the
  Gaul.") and knew Lupus by his hand ("you'll know him by his hand"); a big man with "a stick of olive wood"; a third who held Mara
  ("Be still, mistress. Be good."). On the page they carry one knife (the talker's) and one stick, and the third man uses his hands;
  Valens's point is the missing rope: "Those three brought none." **Inference, not page fact:** that they came on the Lechaion boat.
  The page has only "I told them about it the whole crossing." and, at Kirrha that morning, "A boat from Lechaion had come in on the
  morning wind". Two of them got away, and both heard Valens call "Mara." (Ch.17 may use it, or not). Celer himself never appears and
  is never quoted; keep his on-page voice for Ch.17.

#### POMPONIA GRATA — thermopolium keeper on the Via dell'Abbondanza; the family's landlady and neighbor (Ch.19–40)
- **Attention / worldview:** trade, appetite, gossip, her son's election, money that isn't there. Every
  person is a customer, a debtor or a story.
- **Vocabulary band:** rapid Oscan-flavored Pompeian Latin; food, prices of lentils and wine, street
  names, the names of everyone on the block.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** a torrent of comma-spliced clauses, each one a sale or a complaint. **She tells
  you about yourself and never asks a personal question.**
- **Rhythm:** flood, then a command ("Eat this. Hold that.").
- **Evasion — never says:** anything that would require asking why. She takes the travel money in Ch.38
  and laughs rather than ask. Her husband's dice debts are mentioned only as a joke.
- **Physicality / silence:** **touches people's arms** while she talks. Whitewashes rivals' election
  notices at night, personally. Rests her forearms on the counter over the steaming pots.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (telling, never asking) and physicality (touch)
- **Bleed guard:** must not ask questions the way Felix does, and must not take on Kallia's terse
  imperatives as a rhythm. Grata's commands come at the end of a flood.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the woman who narrates you to your face and never asks you a thing, against
  Kallia, who says almost nothing, and Felix, who only asks.
- **Sample line:** "That pot isn't worth the glue and you know it and you'll mend it anyway, that's your
  trouble, you'd mend a cracked egg if somebody cried over it. Here. Hold this, it's hot."

## THE CHILDREN

#### LUCIUS — son of Mara and Valens; b. summer AD 74 (in arms Ch.25; speaks Ch.27–29, 32, 34, 37–44; age 5 at the eruption)
- **Attention / worldview:** rules and real tasks. He wants to be given a job and takes adults at their
  word. He is Valens's stillness without the fear.
- **Vocabulary band:** Latin first, some Greek from his parents, a few English words from Mara. He calls
  Valens **Tata** (the Latin nursery word for father, made clear by context once) and Mara **Mama**.
- **Age-stage voice:**
  - **About 2 (Ch.27–29):** single words and two-word requests; names objects; watches.
  - **3 (Ch.32):** fever; mostly sound and a few words; a short sentence on waking.
  - **4 (Ch.34):** short whole sentences; questions about how things work and what is allowed.
  - **5 (Ch.37–44):** full sentences, careful and literal. He acts on instructions exactly as given and
    asks one grave question at a time.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** complete, careful sentences; statements of fact; one question, then waiting.
- **Rhythm:** slow and considered for a child; long silences while he watches.
- **Evasion — never says:** that he is frightened. He asks what to do instead. He never whines.
- **Physicality / silence:** still, watchful; the knucklebone rolled and caught; holds a lamp with both
  hands when trusted with it.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (rules and tasks) and rhythm (one grave question,
  then waiting)
- **Bleed guard:** must not quote instructions back as a habit. His literalism shows in what he does, not
  in a repeated verbal form.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the child who asks one serious question and waits, against Aelia, who never
  waits for anything.
- **Sample line (age 5):** "Tata said I could carry the lamp if I walk. I'm walking."

#### AELIA — daughter of Mara and Valens; b. winter AD 75–76 (newborn Ch.26; babbling Ch.29; Ch.30, 34, 38–44; 3½ at the eruption)
- **Attention / worldview:** the world's textures and tastes. She is greedy for everything and finds
  delight where adults find danger (sand, must, floating stones, falling pumice).
- **Vocabulary band:** Latin nursery words; sensory nouns and verbs (hot, wet, sticky, look, mine, more).
  An invented word may occur **once**, never as a refrain.
- **Age-stage voice:**
  - **Newborn (Ch.26):** no speech; loud, grabbing, greedy.
  - **About 1 (Ch.29–30):** babble and laughter.
  - **About 2½ (Ch.34):** single words and commands; eats sand.
  - **3½ (Ch.38–44):** short exclamations and imperatives, a sentence or two at most. "Mama, look"
    (Ch.39) is spoken **once**.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** exclamations, imperatives, nouns with "mine" or "more." She asks no questions
  that she waits to have answered.
- **Rhythm:** quick, loud, then suddenly asleep.
- **Evasion — never says:** "no" to anything new. Nothing frightens her on the page until the door, and
  even there she presses rather than fears.
- **Physicality / silence:** grabs, tastes, presses her face and palms to things (shutters, plaster).
  Sticky with must at the Vinalia. Her fist closes on the knucklebone (Ch.44).
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (touch and taste) and rhythm (exclamation, no waiting)
- **Bleed guard:** must not become a cute-phrase dispenser; no line of hers is repeated.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the child who grabs and exclaims, against Lucius, who watches and asks.
- **Sample line (3½):** "Hot! The bread's hot, feel, Mama, feel it."
