## THE 1998 FRAME

#### EMILIO REYES — Mara's father; field archaeologist, Philadelphia; d. January 1998 aged 68 (Ch.1 outgoing message on tape; past-facing memory throughout)
- **Attention / worldview:** places and strata, meaning where a thing was found and how deep. He locates
  himself and everyone else by *where*. The past was more vivid to him than the people waiting at home.
- **Vocabulary band:** a warm, performing university man's American English; field slang; place names.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** easy, generous, a little theatrical, a speaker used to rooms of students.
  Promises put in the conditional and the season, never the date.
- **Rhythm:** a cheerful run that ends on an invitation.
- **Evasion — never says:** **when** he will be back. He never names a date (contrast Fenn, who names
  dates wrongly). His promise to take her ("next season, you come with me") may be quoted verbatim **once
  in the whole book**; everywhere else it is paraphrased (at-risk list).
- **Physicality / silence:** absent; known by objects (the 1966 Seiko stopped on the nightstand, the
  Pentax, the field notebooks she does not open, the dig slides, the answering machine).
- **Canon guard (foundation §3; synced 2026-10-19 after Ch.7 C-01):** he **never took her abroad**, not once.
  She knows his sites (Sicily, Syracuse, Cyprus) only from his slides and his talk at the kitchen table (Ch.1;
  Ch.7 "from his slides"). No later chapter may give her a trip with him, at any age.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by evasion (never commits to a when) and attention (where)
- **Bleed guard:** Mara's memories of him must not develop a recurring framing line ("My father used to
  say…"). Vary the entry every time.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the man who tells you where he is and never when he'll be back, against Fenn,
  who tells you exactly when he'll be back and is wrong.
- **Sample line (the tape):** "You've got the Reyes house. I'm out in the field, or I'm asleep in the
  chair, and either way leave me your name and I'll call you when I'm back."

#### PROF. HALLORAN — Emilio's permit contact and colleague, phoning from Naples (Ch.1 on the machine; Ch.2 by phone)
- **Attention / worldview:** paperwork, deadlines, permits, the season's logistics, the Soprintendenza.
  Kind in the way institutions are kind.
- **Vocabulary band:** Irish-born academic long resident in Naples; administrative English with Italian
  office words ("the Soprintendenza," "the *permesso*").
- **Syntax fingerprint:** apology, then request; euphemism around death; dates and deadlines given
  precisely.
- **Rhythm:** courteous, slightly over-long, trailing into "well."
- **Evasion — never says:** "died" or "dead." He says "in Emilio's place" and "since January." There is
  **no portent** in his Campanian invitation; it is a neutral fact.
- **Physicality / silence:** a voice on a bad international line; the pause before a hard word.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by evasion (euphemism for death) and attention (paperwork and dates)
- **Bleed guard:** no hint of destiny or of Pompeii as meaningful. He is a man with forms.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only voice that wraps death in paperwork, against Emilio's tape, which
  is all warmth and no dates.
- **Sample line:** "I'm sorry to ring about forms at a time like this, Ms. Reyes, but the permit needs a
  name on it by the twentieth of April, and your father's is, well. It's still the one on it."
- **Ch.2 as written (Writer, 2026-10-18):** rings the house on the last Saturday in March (~10 a.m.
  Philadelphia / 4 p.m. Naples, both still on standard time); the machine "only beeps" (no greeting since
  Ch.1). His own life on the call: he and his wife are leaving their flat after thirty-one years; he sits on
  the floor of an emptied room with the telephone in his lap; the new tenants arrive to measure for
  curtains. The Soprintendenza accept him as director but returned the cession marked *firma mancante*
  (the outgoing director's signature missing); Mara signs an heir's declaration before a notary instead
  (by the fifteenth; the 20th is a Monday). Asks for Emilio's last-season field notebooks (the north
  transects exist only there) "by the end of June". Invites her for the season from 6 July "in Emilio's
  place"; she says she might be in Greece; "you'll practically be next door." Never says "died." Speaks
  Italian to the tenants, not cheerfully.

#### IRENE SZABO — antiques dealer, Pine Street's Antique Row; Hungarian, in her seventies (Ch.1)
- **Attention / worldview:** authenticity: fakes, period accuracy, what a thing will fetch, whether a
  customer is a dealer or a fool. She bought the map only because it is *right* for its period.
- **Vocabulary band:** precise immigrant English with dealer's terms (lot, provenance, estate sale,
  laid paper). Sparing.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** **no contractions;** short present-tense verdicts; statements where others
  would ask questions ("You are not a dealer.").
- **Rhythm:** a verdict, a pause, a price.
- **Evasion — never says:** condolences, sentiment, or any warning or destiny line about the map.
- **Physicality / silence:** looks over glasses she is not wearing; weights a chart's corners with
  whatever is to hand; haggles without moving.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (no contractions, verdicts) and attention (authenticity)
- **Bleed guard:** must not become the mysterious shopkeeper; she wants it off her shelves.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the seller who sells by contempt in very few words, against Grata, who sells
  by flood and flattery.
- **Sample line:** "Fakers make the mountains too tall. They think tall looks old. This one is dull in the
  right places, which is why I paid for it."
- **Ch.1 as written (Writer, 2026-10-18):** buzzer-and-speaker shop door; magnifier pushed up into her
  white braid; liver-colored cardigan, fingerless gloves; a mute TV with a college game and a $5 bet with
  the barber next door (her own life, nothing to do with Mara). Notices the obituary name ("There was a
  Reyes in the *Inquirer*") and lets Mara's "It's a common name" stand. No condolence, no warning.

#### DOUG FESSLER — field archaeologist; dug with Emilio eleven seasons in Cyprus and two in Sicily; gave the eulogy (Ch.1)
- **Attention / worldview:** his own fieldwork and its logistics: his lost duffel, his context sheets,
  the sherd he could not place in 1984. Grief reaches him as anecdote about a site, never as a feeling
  stated.
- **Vocabulary band:** American field-archaeologist shop talk (context sheets, type series, section,
  offprints, dig house), plain and warm.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** runaway anecdote that loops back to correct itself; agrees with himself out loud;
  talks around food in his mouth.
- **Rhythm:** a story, a self-interruption, an offer of practical help ("I can do the office").
- **Evasion — never says:** "died," or anything about his own grief; he cuts the story he would have cried
  at and says so instead of crying.
- **Physicality / silence:** a fresh winter-season sunburn from Jordan with a hat-line across the forehead
  and peeling at the nose; a borrowed tie; a paper plate holding one stick of celery, which he talks to.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (his own site logistics) and physicality (the sunburn)
- **Bleed guard:** must not notice materials or joins (Mara's axis); his sherd talk is about *placing* a
  thing in a sequence, never about how it was made or mended.
- **Chaos on the page (subjects, never phrases):** Royal Jordanian lost his duffel with six weeks of context
  sheets; he cannot stop talking about it at a memorial.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the colleague who mourns through his own anecdotes and luggage, against
  Halloran, who mourns through permits and dates.
- **Sample line:** "Don't look at the nose. Six weeks in a hat and the one day I take it off they're
  pouring concrete."

#### GLORIA — Mara's cousin on her father's side; ~36 in 1998; married to Ray, fourteen years (Ch.1)
- **Attention / worldview:** the family's practical ledger: leftovers, freezers, keys, who called whom,
  who was offended and who had to lie down. She manages grief by assigning errands.
- **Vocabulary band:** Philadelphia family English, quick and contracted, with an occasional Spanish
  kinship word (*Tía*).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** short logistical questions ("Where'd you go?" "How much?" "Did you eat
  anything?") followed by instructions; tells you her own small humiliations as comedy.
- **Rhythm:** a complaint about herself, a question, a list of jobs, a command.
- **Evasion — never says:** Emilio's name. In Ch.1 he is only "him," "his machine," "nobody gets him."
  She never asks Mara how she feels.
- **Physicality / silence:** works with her hands while she talks (foil folded with the heel of the hand),
  stockinged feet with her pumps parked under the coat rack; the long wordless look (Ch.1), held instead of
  a sentence; holds a coat open at a child's height.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by evasion (never names the dead man) and attention (errands and
  the family ledger)
- **Bleed guard:** never Mara's dry aside or her eye for materials; never Grata's flattery or sales talk
  (Gloria asks practical questions; Grata never asks). Her hands-at-work is *domestic packing* only, to stay
  clear of Kallia's labor-attention in Delphi.
- **Chaos on the page (subjects, never phrases):** wore two different earrings (a pearl stud and a gold
  hoop) through the whole memorial; Ray called it a style.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the relative who will not say the dead man's name and hands you a job
  instead, against Halloran, who says the name and wraps the death in paperwork.
- **Sample line:** "Your keys are in the freezer door, don't ask me why, and Ray's waiting, so take the
  pierogi."

#### PAT — Emilio's next-door neighbor (Ch.1, on the answering-machine tape only)
- **Attention / worldview:** borrowed dishes and neighborly favors; who has whose Tupperware.
- **Vocabulary band:** warm South-Jersey-and-Philly neighbor English ("hon," "bye now"), used once.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** flustered self-correction mid-sentence ("your Tupperware, I mean his").
- **Rhythm:** apology, errand, a single remembered kindness, sign-off.
- **Evasion — never says:** why she is calling a dead man's number.
- **Physicality / silence:** none on the page; a voice on the incoming-message tape (erased in Ch.1).
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (self-correction) and attention (lent objects)
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only voice that calls the house by habit and corrects herself on tape,
  against Halloran's deliberate, over-long message.
- **Sample line:** none beyond the Ch.1 tape.

#### Ch.1 mention-only names and unnamed walk-ons (no card needed unless they return)
- **Mentioned by name only:** Ray (Gloria's husband; waits in the car); Tía Nilda (78; forgot, phoned
  Emilio's number to tell him the memorial time and got the greeting); Dorothy (neighbor; masking tape on a
  Pyrex lid: RETURN DISH); Carol (may have baked a lemon cake); Frank (the museum registrar).
- **Mara's mother:** alive, in Phoenix; divorced from Emilio around 1981; sent lilies and a card. Off-page.
- **Unnamed walk-ons:** the dean (jingles his change; "lecture fund"); the dean's-office woman who lifts
  the chafing-dish lids and presses the lemon cake on Mara (her own worry: the smoke detectors and the fire
  department's bill); the loading-dock security guard with a soft pretzel; the cab driver (wife's eleven
  rosebushes, "wrong mulch"); the cheerful caller from Nicosia who has not heard (Emilio's Kourion
  drawings, '92); the department office caller about the memorial room.

---

#### Ch.2 mention-only names and unnamed walk-ons (no card needed unless they return)
- **Mentioned by name only:** Father Kostas (St. George's; in Fenn's 1967 entry only, never speaks). Pat and
  Dorothy recur by mention (Mara asked Pat to watch the house; Dorothy's dish still in the refrigerator).
- **Gloria (existing card) in Ch.2:** phones the house on a Saturday in May from her garage on the cordless
  ("Don't ask"); errands only (mail, taps, Dorothy's dish, the key in the flowerpot, not under the mat);
  "call me when you get there. Collect." Still never names Emilio.
- **Unnamed walk-ons:** the Sansom Street jeweler (telephone clamped to his shoulder the whole visit, arguing
  that a ring is not a ring until paid for; takes three links out of the Seiko's band; $8); the notary at a
  check-cashing place on Baltimore Avenue (offers a lottery ticket); the Olympic Airways agent; the head of
  Mara's department (signs the leave form unread). None has a device; none notices materials.

#### Ch.3 unnamed walk-ons (no card needed unless they return or are named)
- **The German hiker** (the only extended speech of Ch.3): about 65, from Ulm by way of his daughter's
  kitchen; red face, white knee socks, red windbreaker, canvas hat with a chin cord, two walking poles.
  Attention: weather, the trail markings (follow the red; the yellow-and-black E4 is the long way), the
  two o'clock cloud off the gulf. His own life: a stone from every summit for his daughter's windowsill
  (this one from Liakoura, with a fossil shell); a son-in-law who glues cup handles back on badly; a
  wife in Arachova with a bad knee who thinks he takes the bus up. A point-and-shoot Olympus; cannot work
  a manual camera. Syntax: German-English present tense, inverted objects ("Olympus she has"), short
  verdicts. No device, no materials-eye (he describes the cups by what goes wrong, not how they were
  made). "Like the cheese" (Philadelphia) is spent: 2 uses, never again.
- **The goatherd** (Kalivia slope, 1998): old, flat cap, gray suit jacket over a sweater, crook;
  cursing a nanny goat on a ledge, stops mid-word for Mara's "Kalimera," then finishes the curse. (The
  earlier "shouts into a mobile phone" beat was CUT in the Ch.3 disruption pass: a character turning to
  a device instead of Mara had already happened twice — Szabo's TV, the jeweler's phone — and that
  pattern is on the at-risk list now.) Distinct from the AD 70 goat-boy of Ch.4 (different century;
  different person).
- **The Arachova taxi driver** (no speech): blue glass eye-beads on his mirror; laughs at the duffel.
- **Mentioned only:** the Delphi hotel desk woman (asked whether Mara was going back to Athens); the
  museum door guard in Delphi in a blue uniform (tore her ticket on Wednesday; Mara means to hand him the
  knucklebone); E. Wilkins of Bristol, 1861, a name cut in the cave wall (graffiti, not a character).

#### Ch.4 unnamed walk-on and mention-only names (no card needed unless he returns or is named)
- **The goat-boy** (Corycian slope, autumn AD 70; UNNAMED: Mara asks his name and he answers with
  hers). About nine; brown coarse tunic belted with cord; bare feet gray with dust, soles hard as
  saddle leather; a sling round his neck, pouch on his chest; hair knife-cropped; a crust under his
  nose; a stick he keeps low; a black-handled leather bag (white cheese and a heel of barley bread
  in a gray rag). **Attention:** goddess or trouble, then his goats, his thorn, and the shiny thing.
  **Syntax:** rural Phocian Koine rendered in short transliterated bursts (*Ti? Pothen ílthes? Ek
  tou antrou? Móni í? Mí gráfe. Ti dósis? Pheu. Phage. Kalon. Ide. Katō. Makran. Késar en Rómi*),
  imperatives and questions, never a full sentence Mara can follow. Shifted vowels are spelled as
  sound (eta/ei/oi → i, ai → e, upsilon → ü); Mara decodes those words only from gesture or context.
  The word-doubling cadence is HIS alone (Mara's echoes were cut in cycle 1). **Evasion:** his own
  name. **Physicality:** spits into the neck of his tunic against the uncanny (once); rubs out her
  written ΔΕΛΦΟΙ with his foot; laughs in high hiccups when she takes *nümfi* for his name; spits out the granola bar; keeps the
  foil wrapper down his tunic; digs a thorn out of his heel with another thorn while grumbling at a
  goat called Kissa. Says *nymphē* three times (rendered "Nümfi" twice, "Nymphē" on the third, when
  Mara hears it). **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (the uncanny vs. the practical
  goatherd's day) and physicality. No device; he notices no materials or joins. **DISTINCTNESS
  GUARANTEE:** the only speaker in the book whose words reach the page as sound Mara cannot parse,
  against the 1998 goatherd (Ch.3), who speaks one word of modern Greek she understands at once.
  Different century, different person. (If he is ever named, promote to a full card.)
- **Mentioned by name only:** Kleodamos (on the page twice, ¶49 and ¶55: the boy's answer to "who is
  archon": a Delphic eponymous archon, fictional, meaningless to Mara; NOT a year-dating clue; not the
  pen's owner and not the boy's father); the month Boathoos (Delphic,
  roughly Sept–Oct; Mara does not know the Delphic calendar's correlation); Kissa (a goat).
- **Spent in Ch.4 (do not reuse):** "Khaire/Khére" farce; the Theophrastus superstitious-man
  aside; "strangers and beggars are from Zeus" (Odyssey, quoted once); the Wawa hoagie case.

#### Ch.5 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
- **The press woman** (olive farm on the road down from the Arachova village): Mara's age, skirts kilted
  through her belt, forearms oiled to the elbow, skims a sunk jar with a wide shell. **Her own life:** her
  husband has been two days at Kirrha "with the jars" (a word from Aristophanes for a useless husband);
  she rails at her sleeping son and later kicks him awake and hands him the shell. Asks "Where's your
  man?"; pays Mara for carrying olives with bread dipped in new oil. "They charge you for breathing up
  there." Attention: her own short-handed day. No device; notices no materials.
- **The pilgrim family** (father, mother, grown son with a white kid for sacrifice): father, "Where else
  would it go? Up there's the god and down there's the sea…"; son mocks Mara's Attic ("like the man who
  reads out the laws"); mother: "You don't know what she is." Distinct from the Ch.4 goat-boy.
- **The olive girl** on a terrace: laughs at Mara biting a raw olive ("You salt them first! Salt!").
- **The guide** (Sacred Way): lean, about fifty, brown cloak whose brooch has lost its pin and is fastened
  with a thorn; talks walking backward up the paving without looking. **Attention:** the speech and the
  fee; the story, never the maker ("Who made it? Nobody asks that. A man from Aegina, and they paid him").
  **Syntax:** memorized periods worn smooth (Mara can follow them), broken by asides about money and
  rivals. **His own life:** a feud with a rival guide in a green cloak "from Amphissa" who tells the
  Siphnians wrong, and a jab at the wool-seller who washes her wool in the horse trough. Recites the
  absent Kroton runner over an empty base; calls Nero "the singer" and gets his death wrong ("in a
  ditch"); takes Mara's 100-drachma coin, reads its Greek capitals where it lies on his palm (no biting), mimics her Attic to the crowd. That
  laugh is the trigger for Mara's refusal to speak Greek (all-or-nothing). No device; notices no
  workmanship. **Spent:** the recital for an absent statue; the mimicry; "What is a stranger going to look
  at? The holes?"
- **The rival guide in green** (from Amphissa): "I heard that"; "Is it the Pythia's grandmother?"
- **The water boy** ("Spring water, cold, an obol the cup!") and **the wool-seller** (takes up the mimicry and
  sings the end of it after them; no laugh-fit).
- **The temple warden** (white tunic, purple stripe, staff) and two men: "Off the god's step! Who let her
  up here unwashed?" / "Whose is she?" (an unattributed voice among the three) / "Is that Syrian?" / "The rod! Bring the rod!"
- **The temple slave with the rod**: barefoot, thickset, cropped head, a peeled rod as long as Mara's arm.
  **Attention:** order on the steps; commands only. **His own life:** the rod splits on her and he shows
  the split to the warden, who ignores him: "Look at it… Split end to end, and they'll stop it out of my bread." (Distinct
  from the planned Ch.13 temple slave who summons Mara to Theano.)
- **The herald** (agora colonnade): stout, staff wound with white wool, a boy with a wax tablet. Big, slow
  voice that sets down every word (the guide's worn speech comes first; the herald is the first public Greek). **His own life:**
  long-sighted, holds the tablet at arm's length, garbles the imperial titles, is corrected by **the
  clerk** in the doorway ("Autokratōr Kaisar Ouespasianos Sebastos"), repeats it sourly, and answers
  "Citizens only?" with "The rest of you can smell it cooking." Public formula only.
- **The clay-figure seller** (next pillar): moves her board away from Mara, then back; her husband makes
  the mold-made figurines; "Since the bronzes were carted off there's no buying." ("the singer" stays at 2)
- **Two men at the fountain**: four oxen for the whole town (Mara is not sure the herald said any number; she heard "the fourth day"); a brother's wife who got a hoof; "Then that
  god will want paying for it." / "Not from us, he won't."
- **The procurer at dusk**: younger than Mara, good cloak, bad smile; offers "a house down past the
  stadium road that takes in girls on their own." Mara refuses in English.
- **The neighbor with the sieve** (Kallia's lane) and **the pilgrim on the gallery** (asks for lentils;
  "Can she sing?"); **the singer** upstairs with the endless song about a girl from Corinth.
- **Mention only:** Kleodamos (archon) again, in the herald's decree (still a name only). Gloria (the
  ninth of June). Dr. Pinsky, Mara's 1998 dentist on Walnut Street (memory only; told her she brushed too
  hard).
- **Spent in Ch.5 (do not reuse):** "The Mummers had got at it"; "I preferred it dead"; "A pair of
  hands" / "She can carry"; "lamp oil was lamp oil"; the shutter "like a slammed drawer"; the dog "like a
  car alarm on Baltimore Avenue"; Egyptian blue "like ground sugar" on a slide; the Thessalians taken off
  their horses; "Eggs. I can do eggs."; the hundred-drachma coin.

#### Ch.6 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
*(Synced to the final page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair and polish cycle 1.)*
- **The charcoal burner** (top of the cliff path above Delphi, November AD 70): the man with the donkey Valens
  suspects on the way up; that evening he sits up with his kiln (a long hump of earth and turf, smoke seeping from
  every seam) on an upturned basket with a sack across his head and shoulders. Hails them once as "Mule-man!"
  (one use only, so it is not an epithet): "Come and warm yourselves. She won't mind." **His own life:** the kiln,
  nine days burning ("Nine days she's been at it, and now this."), which he talks to as "she"/"girl" ("Easy, girl.
  Hold it in.") and stamps shut where flame shows; a grievance about the god's oxen that runs past Mara ("Did you
  get any of the god's ox?" The rest ran on past me. I caught the number four in it, and the council, and a
  grievance about meat.); to Valens's "I was at Kirrha": "So you ate what we ate. Smoke." Roasts chestnuts at the
  edge of the embers and pushes them to Mara with his foot ("Eat. They're split already."). His cousin's boy is
  the Ch.4 goat-boy, who saw her "step out of Pan's cave at the olive beating"; he says the next part slowly, to
  her ("The boy says you ate his cheese and paid him in a cake made of hay."), which is why she can answer "It was
  oats"; "It's all hay to a boy." Fast mountain Greek: "I lost words out of everything he said, and none of
  Valens's answers." At "She hit Pan" the sack across his shoulders shakes (no laugh line, no bodily aftermath;
  the donkey opens its eyes and shuts them again). "Sleep here by her. You'll not get down in the dark." No
  device; notices no materials.
- **A Theban on Kallia's gallery** ("Kallia! Are those lentils ever coming?").
- **Mention only:** the temple painter the priests hang off the roof each spring "with a pot of red" (came off the
  corner at the last painting, broke his arm, back up in a month: "He's cheap"); "a man I knew" from Philadelphia in
  Asia (Valens); the goat-boy again, by report; (Ray's snow tires and the Larchwood neighbor in the January '94 ice
  storm were CUT from Ch.6, AUTHOR-CP2-2, 2026-10-19; neither is on the page);
  Gloria, unnamed as "a cousin", in Mara's cliff speech (the pearl in one ear and the gold hoop in the other
  through a whole church service; the continuity repair replaced the freezer-door keys, which were a bible sample
  line); Mara's father's watch, wound "every morning since March".
- **Mules (no names by design):** the gray (the lead; Mara rides her and is towed up the cliff by her tail), the
  brown (ate Kallia's pomegranate last winter; not on the trip), a dun (carries the duffel).
- **Spent in Ch.6 (do not reuse):** "the temperature of a person" (the bread); "salted it with conviction"; "like
  a crossing guard who had lost her sign"; "like a glass dipped in sugar"; "like the paint on a fire escape" (the
  layered ice; it replaced the cut "glaze on a cheap plate"); "Where I came in."; "From in there. Not over
  anything."; "Latin is not learned on this road." (Mara's); "A thief doesn't fill the widow's jar"; "Then it'll
  spit oak"; "The shovel comes back"; being towed up the cliff by a mule's tail; "It was the plain truth about
  her" (the Latin mule); "I make the cracks in old pots disappear" (the cliff speech); "Most of it was true, and
  the whole of it was wrong." (the one antithesis; keep it to one); "It's all hay to a boy"; "I lost words out of
  everything he said". (Removed in the sync: "like milk being poured off cream" and "Nobody learns Latin on this
  road", which are not on the page.)

#### Ch.7 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
*(Synced to the final page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and the three
finalize fixes. Every quote below is on the page, checked by script.)*
- **The baker's wife at the women's bath:** soft and heavy, a red clay lamp in her fist; at the bath since before light
  because "his mother has moved in on us for the winter, and that house was never built for two women who both know
  best." Offers to singe Mara's legs with the lamp ("Stand still, I'll singe them for you. It doesn't hurt if you don't
  jump."); "She's let herself go like a widow." (to Kallia's face, unremarked); to Kallia's "where she's from": "And
  where's that, where they've no lamps?" (her lamp, her body-talk); finds the vaccination scar ("Somebody's marked
  her."); teases Kallia that "Everything's over the sea with you" (Kallia once said her Thebans came by ship; they
  walked). Attention: bodies and household feuds. No device; notices no workmanship. Since cycle 1 she does not laugh:
  "The woman with the lamp gave me my arm back." (the bath now ends on Kallia's flat lie and the cow's pox).
- **The girl with the mole** (bath): turns Mara's wrist toward the window ("What's this white, then? Has she had a
  fetter on?"), asks "Is she bought?" / "Is that where they put it, over there?", offers the burnt-horn powder ("Horn.
  Burnt and ground. Goat's is best."), smiling with a full set of sound teeth (Mara's teeth chaos, NOT an
  introduction-by-teeth: she enters by the mole).
- **The bath-keeper's wife** (takes Kallia's coin and eyes Mara over it; no line).
- **The salt-seller** (market): broad, on an upturned measure behind a heap of gray salt with a wooden scoop. Mimics
  nothing; DECLAIMS: "Antigone!" … "She's come down off the stage for a scoop of salt!"; gives more salt than the obol
  buys and pushes the coin back ("Come again tomorrow and ask for pepper. My sister's never seen a play."). At the
  spring, coming down the steps with her own jar on her head, she stops the drover: "Leave Antigone alone." / "She's
  Kallia's." / "Kallia's, and Kallia has a tongue on her and the council's ear, and you've got four donkeys drinking the
  god's water at the wrong end of his basin…" Her grievance is the donkeys at the wrong end of the basin. "Antigone" is
  HER name for Mara: **2 uses in Ch.7 (the shout, the spring), CAP REACHED** — if she returns she calls Mara something
  else or nothing.
- **The sprat-seller** (next stall, never raises her head): "Tell her to bury her brother first, the salt will keep."
- **The man at the fountain** (salt market): laughs along with the row with his eyes on Mara, not on the women; no
  line. Unnamed and unreturned; he does not come asking (audit N-18: keep him so before Ch.9).
- **The drover from the west** (Amphissa or beyond; donkeys; morning wine): "Here's the one that doesn't talk." /
  "Kallia's simple one. Is she simple all over, or only in the mouth?" ("simple" ×2, both here) / "Look at me." /
  "She's nothing to you," … "She's nothing to anybody. Ask round this spring." / "Kallia's what?" Takes her wrist; she
  drops the jar. He is NOT asking after Mara (the Ch.7-8 rule holds): he is a man at a spring.
- **The four men in the latrine** ("Thirty-two the jar at Kirrha … the Jews' war … what have the Jews got to do with
  my—"). **The cheese-man** from the valley villages (no line). **The Thebans**: the bassoon voice on the gallery ("Put a
  cushion under it, and another girl under the cushion!"); "Kallia, is she a goat, that she eats the hedge?"; one of them
  snoring "somewhere along the gallery" on the storeroom night (¶378). **The father with three sons and a boundary
  wall** (council hall): "Some of us have a wall coming down, Sosandros."; while Mara's tongue is on the lentisk
  splinter, "the father behind us was back on his wall" (cycle 1; it replaced a laugh in the queue, so the line pays
  off his grievance as comedy without a laugh). The men in the line no longer laugh at anything. **The clerk's boy**
  (carries the brick).
- **Mention only:** "the girl" who ran off at the vintage (UNNAMED by design; her himation of wet-sand brown, quince
  hair oil, a dark crimped hair in the fringe, a hurried darn); the nurse at a pediatric clinic in West Philadelphia
  (the smallpox vaccination); a friend's husband's gin and tonic on Rittenhouse Square; **Syracuse known only from her
  father's slides** (a quarry deep enough to stand a church in, a fountain full of papyrus and ducks, and "his voice in
  our dark kitchen telling me the gelato there tasted of nothing but cold", which since cycle 1 is where the memory
  ends: he never took her abroad, per foundation §3); the offprints from Nicosia printed "Aimilios Reyes", one pinned
  to the corkboard by the telephone; Mrs. Dunphy (see her card; Ch.7 surfacing done). **Mara's Philadelphia building**
  (¶378, recalled in the dark, never revisited): a street door, a vestibule with mailboxes, hers so marked ("mine had my name
  inked on a bit of tape"), stairs, a second landing (her flat is at least two flights up; ENTITY_STATE carries the facts); and
  the counters where she gave the address, "at the dry cleaner's and the circulation desk". (Removed in the syncs: the
  young man in the good cloak past the running track, whose paragraph the disruptor cut; a travel memory that broke
  canon, removed by the continuity repair, C-01; the dry closing sentence after the slides memory, cut in cycle 1.)
- **Spent in Ch.7 (do not reuse):** "a piece of luck lying in the road for whoever bent down first"; "the way a clerk at
  a counter wanted a zip code"; "heavy and not new"; "quince or something with ambitions to be quince"; "bagged into
  the shape of another woman's arms"; "She gave her lessons"; "like a woman carved on a grave"; "You sit like you paid
  for the bench"; "stood square before me"; "In here you look at the pots"; "The price of oil stopped"; "They looked me
  over for weight and for flaws"; the cow's-pox aside; "a different word with the same lentils in it"; "correct was an
  accent like any other"; Antigone / bury your brother; "the lane gave way back"; "There's no such country." / "Then
  why aren't you in it?"; "Hers was a question I could not answer by doing something."; "the knot standing up like a
  little fist"; "the boots took themselves off"; the green weed "combed all one way by the water" on the lion's lip; "I
  had heard the boat invented"; "a city tied onto it"; "half the stand had to help him get a cup of water"; "as flat as
  Philadelphia"; "a Nereid or a star with four legs"; "It was the size of nothing, and it took up my entire mouth.";
  "back on his wall"; "the boundary wall came forward with its three sons"; "the least lasting surface in Delphi";
  "light at both ends"; "the hedge was better company than he was"; "burnished slip scoured to a dull fog"; and from the
  storeroom scene (¶378, cycle 1 + finalize): "English went with the rest"; "how much of it was already gone"; "across a
  hundred counters"; "After the two letters came nothing."; "A one came, and then a nine, and after the nine only
  guesses."; "I had to go back for the zip code, the long way."; "my name inked on a bit of tape"; "the figures turned
  up again in their right order"; "kept even my own company in Greek"; "a woman I had to think about before I knew her".
  (Removed in the syncs, so no longer on the page and not quoted here: the disruptor's cut line about a stripped woman at the
  bath; the earlier sea-nymph wording of the Nereid gloss, W-05; and three lines cut in cycle 1: the dry kicker closing
  the slides memory, the exclusivity wording of ¶190 (now "Hers was a question…"), and the old ¶376 summary clause about
  giving up English in the storeroom.)

#### Ch.8 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
*(Synced to the final page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair and polish cycle 1. Every quote
below is on the page, checked by script.)*
- **The stranger in the goat-hair cloak** (the woman Mara has never spoken to; UNNAMED by design): big, sitting with her
  feet out and "a black goat-hair cloak round her like a tent, the shepherds' kind, with the snow lying unmelted on its
  shoulders". "I would not have known her face." Her left sandal strap "had been patched over the instep with a square of
  paler leather, sewn on with a thong and set on askew" (continuity W-14: no knot; Mara knows the feet, not the face: "I
  had studied that patch at the spring every morning for weeks, while the woman who wore it stood beside me and talked
  about the cold, and I had given her nothing but the top of my head"). Keeps her sandals by her own feet; chews something
  she keeps in a fold of her cloak, slowly, with her eyes shut (the negation pivot before it was cut);
  "Not that one." … "Mine are here." (without opening her eyes); "Even the big woman had opened her eyes" when the verse
  stops. **Attention:** weather and warmth, comfortably; she talks to strangers. In the heap: "You," … "You at the
  lions." / "Each morning I tell you about the weather, and each morning you look at my feet." / "You're letting the cold
  in at this end." "She took my wrist and pulled" (the softening adverb was cut in cycle 1; still an unremarked inversion of the Ch.7
  drover's grip at the same spring, and the narrator no longer points at it: the wrist coda was cut) and raises her cloak:
  "Come here, and bring your feet." Under it: "her heat, a great store of it, and the coarse black hair of the cloak
  against my face" (no smell, no breath at the neck since cycle 1). "It was worse the winter the spring froze," … "The
  lions had ice in their mouths a hand long. We broke it with a hammer to get at the water." / "This? This is nothing.
  You should have been up here the time before last. We had a woman lose the end of her nose." … "Not that she ever used
  it much." She works her knees in behind Mara's; "Soon she went heavy against my back, and she was asleep." Mara's whole
  answer: "It is very cold." If she is ever named, promote to a full card.
- **The old woman with the winnowing basket** (the *liknon*): "a broad shallow scoop of woven reed with a cloth tied over
  it", carried on her head up the cliff "without putting a hand to it"; lodges it on Philista's rock; unties
  the cloth (a round loaf, a wrinkled apple, a twist of raw wool, a sprig of ivy); "She lifted it in both hands." / "He's
  heavy this year." The toss is "the winnowing toss, a lift and a jerk to let the wind take the chaff" (cycle 1 cut the
  summer-threshing gloss Mara could not have seen). No other line.
- **The tall woman in the fawnskin** (dappled side out, its little black hooves knocking together at her hip; "tawed with
  the fat still on the belly", stiff as card and "splitting where the ribs had been").
- **The girl who brings the fire up from the pot**; **the woman at the front with fire in a pot**; **the two younger women
  who go down the stair** ("The next bend's gone," … "there was none."; the other comes back "on her hands and knees");
  **the crying mother** ("My two are with his mother. She'll have them in her bed with the dog.") and **her neighbor**
  ("They'll be warmer than you," … "and glad of a night without your feet in their backs."); the women asking about the
  long way ("Could we go down the long way? By the villages?" / "In this? You'd be under a drift by the second village,
  and the shepherds' dogs would be the ones to find you."), a light ("Give me a light, someone. Mine's gone out." /
  "Mine's going. Hold it close to mine, then, and don't breathe on it."), and a brother bringing the men ("My brother will
  bring the men up the stair in the morning"); **the women comparing feet** in the pen ("Look at that. I've three toes
  gone white." / "Mine are blue." / "Put them on Timo. She's warm; she's still burning from the wedding." / "You said a
  man," … "Up here." / "I said husband. A husband isn't a man."); **a woman behind Nikaso, "nobody I knew"** (stops
  wringing out her rag to look); **the farce voices** ("Who put a stone in the ring?" / "That's mine." / "Yours had a nail
  in the heel. Feel it. That's no nail." / "Who's got the one with the strap through the toe?" / "You've two right feet on
  you, girl. Look at them.": the chapter's one "girl" as address, unattributed).
- **Men on the street** (some hold torches over the street to see their wives and daughters off); **a boy in the gutter**
  shouting his mother's name until she cuffs him home; **a Theban** at a shutter ("Bring us back some of the god's wine,
  Kallia, and we'll pay you the month early."; cycle 1 took the joke off lentils, and the Theban heckle is now CLOSED).
- **Mention only:** Nikaso's husband (the man with the sieve, Ch.5; "He takes them off for the bed") and **his sister**
  (owner of the red sandals; "She'll have my eyes"; "She's never been to Kirrha."); **Nikaso's mother** (in her stair
  story, "when my mother was a girl"); Timo's husband (a potter's son from the lower town, married at the vintage) and her
  mother (the child's sandal) and his mother; Sostrata's husband (the baker, twenty years) and his mother (the dough);
  Philista's man ("went off to the Athenians"); the Athenian women (not come: "The roads are shut, they say."); the god in
  the basket ("Your brother's gone away for the winter and left you the house"); Euripides (the cry); Prometheus (the
  fennel pith); "a bank downtown" (the disruptor removed the intersection). (The Vermont brothers at Twenty-second
  and Pine were CUT, AUTHOR-CP2-2, 2026-10-19.)
  Plutarch is no longer named or remembered on the page: the frozen cloaks are Nikaso's story.
- **The baker's wife from the Ch.7 bath is named here: SOSTRATA** (card below). The Ch.7 walk-on note stands for her bath
  scene.
- **Spent in Ch.8 (do not reuse):** "I left my hand in there until I had been fair to it."; "There was nobody to laugh.";
  "sent my hand in to the wrist"; "January cold and no more"; "changed it for my last pine"; "The rock had been unmarked
  since it was rock"; "scored it like the bottom of a saucepan"; the shovel scratches as "the only new thing in the cave";
  "Down on the plain Nikaso would be drinking my share."; "It's a cave," … "It's always open."; "There is no he."; "so
  small," … "that I lost him on the boat"; "I have made him bigger for the song"; "Knead it, knead it, the oven's gone
  cold!" (quoted twice; CAP REACHED, never sung again); "the smoke kept changing its mind"; "mostly for the sake of my
  feet"; "my mouth made it Delphian"; "once from the drum and once off the rock"; "not one turn unsettled it"; "landed in my stomach
  like a coal"; "like a party through the wall of the next apartment"; "like a teacher at a classroom door after the
  bell"; "like a cook banging a pot to bring the household in"; "like flatbreads on a griddle"; "like water over a sill";
  "like sand thrown at a window"; "as warm as a laundromat in February"; "broke along the fold like a biscuit"; "it sat on
  her toes like a hat"; "a whole ring of the same sandal"; "fifty-nine sandals and a stone" / "An Athenian,"; "Wake up,
  little one. Your brother's gone away for the winter and left you the house."; "It's bread as well"; "She'll have my
  eyes" (Nikaso, CAP 2) / "Now I'll have one eye"; "You're smoking," … "It's not the same thing."; the cloak standing on
  its hem by itself, "with nothing inside it"; "A husband isn't a man."; "Husbands," / "On my cliff, and the god not
  woken."; "He comes to whoever's awake"; "Who would I send in?"; "I'll not lay you out on a stair."; "Some child's
  hopping home barefoot for want of this."; "Any woman who sleeps by herself tonight wakes up dead"; "He can't hear you
  from Delphi. We can."; "Each morning I tell you about the weather"; "the whole of what I had said to her in a month of
  mornings"; "I had given her nothing but the top of my head"; "waited to be told where I went"; "Somebody's hand came
  across in the dark"; "the last roof in Delphi"; the Christmas-tree oil drum (now off the page, CUT 2026-10-19, but still spent); the fennel pith that smokes before it
  flames, with the Prometheus aside (once); Nikaso's frozen-cloaks story on the stair (told once, CLOSED; the callback is
  "I had taken Nikaso's story on the stair for nerves"). (Removed in this sync, because they are no longer on the page:
  the farmyard-chore gloss on the basket, cut by the disruptor; the aniseed and licorice-store simile for the fennel, the
  restaurant-tray simile for the basket, the oven-kitchen simile for the cloak (now the laundromat), the possessive form of Philista's Athenian
  joke (now "An Athenian,") and Philista's "girl" in "He can't hear you from Delphi", all cut in cycle 1 or before.)

#### Ch.9 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
*(Writer, 2026-10-19. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1
and the bending fix with finalize lines F1–F4. Every quote below is on the page, checked by script.)*
- **The letter-cutter** (on "a plank laid across two trestles" at the polygonal wall, "floured all over with stone dust, with a
  mallet in one fist"): **attention** = letters and what he is paid for them; his grievance is men who cut their forebears'
  names to save letters ("He made me leave his grandfather off. Saved himself the letters, and now he reads like something that
  grew in a field." / "And son of Xenon, it ought to say, which is a whole word I've not been paid for." / "They all do it. A
  man frees a girl and haggles with me over his own grandfather."). Mara supplies the legal point ("So that nobody can say she
  bought herself.") and he caps it ("And the god doesn't want a girl to cook for him, so she's free."). He cuts her question
  off ("And if he sells her before then? To some man in Amphissa who has never—" / "She brings him up here and stands him in
  front of it, and he reads it, and his money's gone."). The wall as proof, through his trade ("A tablet gets lost, or a man
  says it did. I've never had to cut one of these twice."), and Aphrodisia's depth ("She paid me an obol over the price, from
  what was left in her pot, to cut hers deep. She can't read a letter of it. I read it out to her the day I finished, twice
  through, and she listened to the whole of it and went home."). Opens with "Mind yourself down there," … "That's a soft block.
  It flies." Directs Mara to Euthydikos ("Any of them would tell you, and add a bit." / "His name's at the foot of half this
  wall; he likes it in stone."). No comparison of his trade to anything; he never notices materials (the "soft block" that
  "flies" is footing for his chisel, not workmanship). His tool business was cut to three beats (dust blown past her face, the
  flake that "ticked away down the steps", the mallet tilted at the Way).
- **Aphrodisia** (named in the manumission document; glimpsed once): "A young woman was passing along the Way with a basket of
  washing on her head, in a tunic dyed a cheap blue that had faded in streaks", "near enough to touch, with her gaze on the
  steps". No line. Home-born in Xenon's house; cannot read. Promote to a card if she returns.
- **Euthydikos's household:** **the slave at the door** (shelling beans; "looked at my feet first, as everyone in Delphi did,
  and then worked his way up"; "The temple's up the hill," / "Everybody has a question." / "Kallia's Syracusan," … "She wants
  the god."; "the slave had none for him, having no beans left to examine"); **the smith** (short, leather apron, eyebrows "long
  since gone into somebody's fire"; speaks to Mara's face, against the priest who will not: "Your grandfather bought it cast
  too thin," … "and the clay's nearly on." / "I know it's lead," … "I put it there." / "Nine years back, for your father. It's
  held, hasn't it?" / "I'd have put it on again. It's no trouble to me." / "And you know lead, do you?" / "Wax." … "On the god's
  bronze. It'll stand till the first warm day and then lie down in the incense." / "Call it two," the smith told his clay (the
  chapter's one "[told] the [non-listener]" tag outside the bees) / "Three drachmas, and the clay found." / "When it lies
  down," … "send to me. I'll bring the lead."); **the smith's boy** (bellows worked "with both knees"; "The boy gaped at me over
  the bellows." The bellows no longer stop when Mara speaks: cycle 1 cut the stranger-freeze); **the bee-man** (old slave who
  keeps the priest's bees "in a row of clay hives along the back wall and cared for little besides"; sits on an overturned
  basket and "talked to them in an undertone"; "Now and again he rose and laid his ear to the end of one." (once; the second
  ear beat is now "The bee-man was smoking a hive."); to the hives, tagged once: "There's a woman at the master's bronze," he
  told them, … "From Syracuse. Don't mind her."; "She's cut herself." … "On his bronze. Don't tell him."; "From them?" … "They'll
  want to know what for." (Mara: "Tell them it is for the god."), and the wax arrives by result only ("In the morning he brought
  me a yellow cake of it wrapped in a fig leaf"; C-01 cut "He went and told them" as a fourth command echo); "Out it comes," …
  "He'll not know the difference. He couldn't find his own feet in that beard." / "He'll look at it in the morning," … "When the
  sun's on the court. He said the sun." / carries the mistress's message. **"the bee-man" ×14 on the page: WATCH** (a role label
  used as a name; if he returns for the bowl, name him and add a card)); **the mistress** (the priest's wife, "much younger than
  the priest", himation kept over her head in her own house, hair "crimped in rows as regular as the flutes on the stand"; first
  "nothing but a hem at the balustrade, and the hem stayed perfectly still", then "Above us the hem was at the balustrade again."
  at the payment; "She came and stood over it."; shows Mara the handle of her mother's bronze mirror, "a little woman standing on
  a tortoise with her arms raised to hold up a disc, and no disc"; "Does it hold?" / "He wants it in its place for the god's
  return." / "The rest is upstairs. It came off on the stair at midwinter. It was my mother's." / "Not now." … "Not in his
  court."; folds "the napkin over the little woman's face"; her message: "when you come for the bowl, come round to the kitchen
  door."). If the mistress returns for the mirror, she gets a card.
- **At the spring:** **the boy at the culvert** (fishing in the mud at the drovers' end of the basin, "lying on his belly with
  one arm down it"; "He scrambled up with something gray and folded in his fist"; one question at a time, every one about money:
  "Is there money in it?" / "What's it say?" / "Which weaver?" / "There's no Glykera at the spring." / "Is it worth anything?";
  Mara sends him to the smith with it and he runs off "holding it out in front of him"); **the women at the lions** (one speaks:
  "Put that where you found it," … "It's been given to the ones below, and they don't want it handed round."; "None of them would
  look at it").
- **In the square:** **the goat man** (pens by the fountain; eating a heel of bread; "'For the god?' he said, with his mouth
  full."; "It's a shaker you're after."; demonstrates the shiver with a handful of trough water: "There. Like a bride. Mine all
  do it; I keep them warm, so the water surprises them."; his rival "out beyond the stadium"; "He looked at the rag in my fist,
  which was the shape of what was in it." "More than that." / "For that you can have the shake and not the goat." / "Send
  whoever's leading her up, with the money. I'll keep her warm till then." (the man assumed, never said)); **the men at the
  board** (five or six, a chorus by design: "They talked across one another, and what reached me came in pieces."; "Five
  hundred drachmas." / "At Corinth. You'd have to walk him to Corinth for it, every step." / "Walk a Thracian to Corinth? He'd
  walk you into the gulf." / "For three hundred I'd give up my landlord, and hold the rope." / "When did that go up?" / "With the
  oil from Thebes. First carts since the snow, and the clerk had it on the wall before the oil was off." / "Lupus. What sort of
  name is that for a man?" / "It's a wolf." / "A Roman will call a slave anything. My cousin's master in Patrae called his cook
  Victory." / "There's a Thracian sells eels at Kirrha." / "That one's sixty, and he couldn't take a kid off its mother."; they
  drift off "toward the money-changer"); **a carter** (oil jars roped in straw, "the carter swearing at his ox").
- **Mention only:** Xenon son of Euangelos (Aphrodisia's former owner, "He's forty, and eats for two."); Damon son of Aristion
  (guarantor); the priest Nikandros and the private witnesses Babylos, Eudoros, Philon son of Philon; Kleodamos (archon,
  recurring); Euthydikos's grandfather (gave the stand) and father (the smith's earlier job); Glykera (the cursed weaver; W-08
  renamed her from "Thraitta" to break the Thracian wink before the notice), Hermes below and Earth; Quintus Arrius Celer (by
  the notice only; posted at Corinth "in the month Dadaphorios", reaching Delphi "With the oil from Thebes"); the goat man's
  rival "out beyond the stadium"; a cousin's master in Patrae and his cook Victory; the Thracian eel-seller at Kirrha; the
  hardware store off Fortieth Street and its cat.

#### Ch.10 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
*(Writer, 2026-10-19. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, hook-craft, the disruptor, continuity repair,
polish cycle 1 and the PATH fix; every quote below is on the page, checked by script.)*
- **The pilgrim from Elis ("the Elean")** — passing figure who will take Ariston's room: "a young man with a peeling scalp who had
  been sick on the boat from Aigion and wanted it known"; holds the letter out "between two fingers"; drinks half the dipper "and
  returned the dipper with the rest still in it." **Attention** = what he is owed and where he will sleep. Everything he knows,
  the wine-seller told him ("he said" relays: 2 after dialogue-polish; his habit inside this one scene, not a house style for
  carriers). Lines: "The wine-seller at Kirrha had it on a nail behind his jars," … "Since the autumn. It came over from Corinth on
  a coaster at the back end of the sailing, and then the sailing stopped, and nobody he'd trust was bound for Delphi. Whoever let
  rooms near the lions would stand me a cup for carrying it, he said." / "Syria. He said it might be bad, a letter that sat so
  long." / "He said a cup." / "In Elis a cup has wine in it." / "Have you a room? I'm here till the god opens his house." (no
  fixed number of nights: continuity C-01) / "I'll give two obols a night." / "Three, then, for anywhere with a roof on it. I was
  sick over the side from Aigion to Kirrha, and I've slept two nights on a deck with a Corinthian's goat." … "Three obols." "He
  went away toward them, coughing." Later "at the lions telling everybody" (Nikaso). Kallia lets him the room "at the three
  obols". **If he returns as the lodger (Ch.11+), name him and give him a card**; his cough and his seasickness are texture, not
  tics, and must not be his only traits.
- **The man from Patrae** (spelled as in Ch.6 and Ch.9; continuity W-06), a lodger "who had stayed behind with a bad foot": "came
  down late for his bread, and she gave it him twice over; he took it twice and kept his mouth shut." No line.
- **The lodgers** ("Every room on the gallery had been taken that week by people come early for the god."; "nearly all of them
  had climbed to the sanctuary to stare at the temple"; "Behind her the gallery was hung with other people's cloaks."; "A lodger's
  tunic was drying on the rail above us."; "A pair of wet sandals stood outside one of them with their soles to the sun."). The
  Thebans are not on the page (gone with the thaw, unstated).
- **Mention only (from the letter and Kallia's answers):** **Shalmath** (Ariston's wife; the writer's first name for her was replaced by
  continuity W-03 to remove a Julia Domna wink) and her mother ("Her mother says it will come before the middle of the winter");
  **Iamblichos** (Shalmath's father, "who sells horses to the cohort"; "Her father has no son, and he calls me his."; Kallia's "The
  horse-seller," … "His name again."); the prefect; the baker and Sostrata, "and his old mother, if she is living" ("She's
  living."); **Philista** ("whose drum it was I put my foot through"); **Thrasyllos** ("who let me take his mules to the harbor";
  dead "when the olives were in, the year after"; Ariston took the mules at ten "and told nobody, and Thrasyllos walked to Kirrha
  after him in his bare feet"); "Glauke's girls at the spring, both of them" ("Glauke's girls are married. The big one's in
  Amphissa with a potter, treading his clay for him."); Nikaso's husband; the Kirrha wine-seller; the Corinthian with the goat on
  the deck; the ox-driver ("a man to drive them, since I cannot"; "It says only a man."). Heard, in the lane at the close: "a
  woman was shaking her blanket over the neighbor's wall, and the neighbor was telling her where else she might shake it." (this
  replaced the goat-driving complaint, a sample-12 slot). Mara's 1998 side: none since the Van Pelt postcard was CUT (AUTHOR-CP2-2,
  2026-10-19); Homs, the Orontes and the Lebanon are present map-knowledge, not a memory.

#### Ch.11 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
*(Writer, 2026-10-20. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20; every quote below is on the page, checked by script.)*
- **The yard dog** — yellow; "lay on the dry side of the gutter, watching it go"; leaves the yard when Felix forks the stalls; goes
  with Valens to bar the gate at the close. Texture; Felix's foil three times; rest it.
- **The black** (a young jenny of the string; mules stay unnamed on the page) — "four years old and sound, or she was" (Felix); comes
  back lame on the near fore, "nodding at every other step"; an abscess ("a black spot near the toe, a lentil's worth"; "a thread of
  gray matter welled from the sole, with a smell I could taste") pared out "in slivers" and poulticed with hot bran (not a "gravel
  track": that was the draft); "She'd remember it."; "All week she had swung her rump at him, and kicked his bucket over every
  evening, and stood on his foot whenever he gave her the chance."; the anchor. **The gray** appears once, not as Valens's frame: the
  matted breastband had "rubbed a bare place on the gray's chest that I had found and the gray had not forgiven me for finding."
- **Praxias's quail** — a fighting bird "bred … for courage" in Phocis, "the expression of a toll collector at the end of a shift";
  blinded "the Amphissan's bird in one eye" (Felix). Texture.
- **The Cilician** (the slaver who brought Felix "from Side to Kirrha" and sold him on the quay; "gave him a drachma back") and
  **somebody selling eels** (the only witness to Felix's freeing). Whether that is the Ch.9 board's sixty-year-old Thracian eel-seller
  is not said and must not be winked at.
- **The Amphissan** (his bird lost an eye to Praxias's quail; his "new bird" loses to the baker's), **the baker** (Felix: "The baker
  feeds his bird on wine, everybody says so."; Praxias "called the baker a thief"), **the wine-seller under the plane tree** (Praxias
  "told the wine-seller the Syracusan had haggled him down to nothing") — all in Felix's report.
- **The boxers "before the god sits"** (Felix's rumor): "A Thessalian, and a man from Elateia who killed somebody at Thebes, they
  say".
- **Mention only, in Felix's boasts and gossip:** a fowler with a hawk "down at the harbor"; "a
  sailor" who told him "in Syracuse they eat octopus raw".
- **Pilgrims on the Kirrha road:** "a family of Achaeans with a grandmother in a basket chair slung between two poles" (polish cycle
  1, ENTITY CH11-5: Achaeans off the gulf boats, not Boeotians); those without a roof camped on the terraces ("a child was crying in
  Thessalian, which sounded much like crying in anything else"); "Some Thessalians, lost" (Felix from the gate).
- **The goat-seller in the square** (Ch.9's goat man, by situation, not re-described: "wants more for his she-goat than I have";
  Valens: "He prices for pilgrims.") and **the man at Kirrha** who "brings them over from Aigion on the boats, at half his price, and
  he lets you pick your own out of the pen" (Ch.12's goat source; name him and card him if he speaks).
- Mention only: "a slave of the god's with a rod" sorting the crowd at the door; the priests who "go down to the spring to wash, in
  the dark"; "Men off the islands selling figs nobody ought to buy"; "Boeotians at the back, drunk by noon" (the sanctuary crowd, in
  Valens's mouth; stays).

#### Ch.12 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
*(Writer, 2026-10-20. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20 (polish cycle 1 + F1–F2; continuity W-22): every quote below is on the page, checked by script.)*
- **The talker (dead)** — "He was short and sunburned, and the dust of the plain was on the hem of his cloak. He had a pleasant,
  creased face"; "He kept pace beside us with his hands behind his back." (the knife hand hidden); was on the benches at Corinth, "I
  had two drachmas on the Gaul." … "I've forgiven you."; "you'll know him by his hand"; "*Rudiarius*"; "And good day to you, lady,";
  dies in the ditch; a new sandal "with the thong tied in a bow across the foot". "It was still a pleasant face." Never named.
- **The big man** — introduced as "One of the other two"; "He was big, and he carried a stick of olive wood, and he had no eyes for me.
  He was watching the back of Valens's right hand."; "My brother's a butcher," … "He's got two like that."; the stick across Valens's
  shoulders; runs up the bank into the olives.
- **The third man** — behind them ("His sandals on the stones."); "laughed, low, at nothing"; wine and sweat; "Be still, mistress. Be
  good."; "It caught him above the ear." (the Pentax); "He walked into the wall, and slid down it."; runs "holding his head together in
  his two hands". (Both survivors are free to report to Corinth; not winked at.)
- **The Aigion dealer** (Ch.11's "man at Kirrha") — "The Aigion man kept his goats in a pen of hurdles at the root of the mole"; the
  goats "had crossed the gulf in one and were not yet sure of the ground"; "The Aigion dealer told Valens his price."; Mara pays. Does
  not speak on the page.
- **The sprat woman at the mole** — "sprats a woman was frying at the mole and selling in twists of fig leaf"; Valens paid her for all
  three.
- **The oil men** — brought his jars along the mole "and argued with him about the straw".
- **The lodgers at Kallia's** (mention only, the reason the camera rides under her clothes) — "Thessalians and Achaeans and a priest's
  cousin from Elateia", sleeping "along Kallia's gallery in rows".
- **The graybeard's party** — "a gray-bearded man leading the donkey, two women behind with baskets on their heads, and a boy":
  "There's a dead one below," … "Somebody's had his purse." / "It's been like this since the freedom. Robbers off every boat." /
  "You've been in the thorns." / "My son's run back to Kirrha for the headman and a cart," … "Somebody ought to sit with him till they
  come, and it won't be me." The younger woman talks of the sandals on him, "how new they were, what a shame"; the boy goes last,
  "looking back at the goat". The son, the headman and **the cart** (the chapter's last image) are off-page.
- **Traders on the climb** — "Traders came down from the town against us, going to sleep at Kirrha for the boats"; "each of them said
  good evening and got good evening back."
- **A man with a tray of lamps on his head** — asked "whether he would reach Kirrha by nightfall." / Valens: "If you don't stop,"
- **Spicula** (NAMED in Ch.12; the consultation goat; an animal, so a walk-on entry like the black and the yard dog, not a four-axis
  card) — "a brown she-goat with a white blaze", from the Aigion pen, "the one that had put her head into his stomach", chosen by Felix
  for it ("She's got a temper. The god likes a temper."); eats "every grain" of the barley in the road during the fight; "She stood at
  the foot of the climb with her head low." and lay down; carried up on Felix's shoulders; asleep at the close, "and her jaw kept
  moving". Named by Felix after Nero's murmillo ("After the murmillo. The one the emperor gave a house to."); **Spiculus himself is NOT
  named on the page.** Mara: "She is the god's," / Felix: "The god won't mind what she's called." Kept at the yard: "The goat comes to
  the yard the evening before." Her butting, her temper and the moving jaw are each used once. Ch.13 does not use her name at all
  (0; she is "the goat" throughout), and has no joke and no repeated goat business.
- **Named in Ch.12, mention only (no card):** **Plautia** (first named here; see her card); **Orestes** (Mara's aside: "Apollo had been
  taking money from men with blood on them since Orestes"); **the Calf-Bearer** (Mara's comparison, "in the museum on the Acropolis");
  **Vespasian** (on the denarii: "some so new that Vespasian's heavy face had hardly been rubbed"). Places and dates first named here:
  Kenchreai, Lechaion, the month Bysios ("It was three days to the seventh of Bysios.").
- **Mention only, unnamed:** the emperor (Nero) and the Gaul; the lanista; the magistrate; Celer's steward; "the girl who did her
  hair"; Plautia's mother; "the Egyptian goddess" at Kenchreai; the fisherman; the Corinthian mule dealer; Plautia's brother and his
  freedman; "a bird was saying the same three notes" in the olives (texture).

#### Ch.13 unnamed walk-ons and mention-only names (no card needed unless they return or are named)
*(Writer, 2026-10-20; SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ3; every quote below is on the page, checked by script.)*
- **The jug boy** (no age given) — at the altar "A boy stood by him with a bronze jug beaded with cold."; sent for more water, "the boy went
  at a run" (the chapter's one gait exit by the gate) and comes back "under a full jar". In the afternoon at Kallia's gate: "I knew him for
  the boy from the altar, who had run for the jar. The front of his white tunic was dark with spilled water"; a cloth-covered basket on his
  arm; he "saved his longest look for me". "The Syracusan," … "The woman that asked about a road. Theano wants her." / "Now. She said now,
  before her knees set." / "Her by herself," to Valens; the rest comes fast and Mara half-catches it: "no men" … "muleteer" … "something
  about the crows". Walks "in the middle of the street, well clear of the eaves", and lifts "a corner of the cloth to make sure of whatever
  was under it". Boasts of labor, not fame: "Forty-one jars," … "Since the dark. Spring to altar and back, and the jug besides. Your goat
  took a jug and a jar. Most take the one jug." … "The Thessalians' ram took two jars, and then it ran." / "He received it after they caught
  it." Leaves "was off downhill, glad to." (no gait). Distinct from Felix by attention (jars, the god's errands), not questions.
- **The altar priest** — "a thin man with his himation pinned high on one shoulder"; pours the jug twice, "spoke short to the boy", then
  empties a full jar along the goat's back "from the fillet to the tail, in one long cold sheet"; "told the terrace in a loud voice that the
  god was receiving, as if somebody might want to argue the point." (the chapter's one "as if"). **The priests at the rock basin** before
  light, heard more than seen: "a man praying through his teeth, and another man telling him the god had heard enough and to get his tunic
  on before he froze in it".
- **The prophetes** (Valens's report only) — stands between the consultant and the screen "sideways, so he can watch both", reads the
  question off the wax "in an ordinary voice", holds a flat hand up "to the screen" while she talks, then gives his own (longer, metrical)
  version; glosses it for a Syracusan as "Her own mountain" ("The one that burns behind her city, with the vines up its sides."); offers
  Valens a question "on my own account".
- **Euthydikos** (offstage, named by Kallia) — "A priest at a table wrote it. Beard cut square."; peers nose-close at Valens; asks "was it
  the mender"; Theano's "the square beard". **His bean-shelling slave** ("the slave who shelled beans on the stool by Euthydikos's bay
  tree", Ch.9's, by situation) "came along below the wall, looking up into each woman's face in turn" and brings the bowl summons: "You're
  Kallia's," … "The mender." / "The bowl wants doing when the god's finished for the year. The one with the lead in it. You're to come up
  when you're sent for." / "He had a look at me, and at the women round me, who were all listening." / "You'll be sent for." The Ch.9 OPEN
  THREAD is **deflected, not accepted**: "The lead has held it this long," … "Another year will do it no harm." and "Before the bowl's turn
  came I meant to be gone." The thread stays open.
- **The woman from Amphissa** — sent her husband in about a well: "North of the house, or south," … "South is where his mother planted her
  fig." (the chapter's one in-law joke) / "He'll put it so the god says south," … "To spite me. You'll see." / "Roads are easy," ("comforted
  on my account") "He likes a road." / "She found him and gave him some thought." / "Is he yours?" / "I said that about mine," … "and look
  where it got me." / after "south": "North, then," … "I'll dig it at night, and tell him the god changed his mind." Her husband "walked
  straight past her to the men he had climbed with" and told them first (the men-tell-men-first observation, once). Her other neighbor:
  "North," … "Always north. The south dries out."
- **The Elateian boxer** (Ch.11's rumor, now seen) — "wore his laurel on top of ears like two folded pancakes, and was praying very hard
  over a cake"; lets Felix feel his knuckles; "too thin" … "come back when I've eaten" (reported). **The Boeotian in the temple** (Valens's
  report) — "wouldn't stop praying"; "ask a ship" at Syracuse; went to sit in the iron chair "and the rods moved him"; "He said a chair was
  a chair."; his own answer: "plant olives down the boundary and let the trees argue it". **The man with the cough** by the stone.
- **The bandaged Phocian boy** — "a boy with his first beard", a strip of linen about his head with "a dark stain on the linen" ("The stain
  on the linen was wine."); "I'm a Phocian,"; his friend, "delighted, with an arm round his neck": "Fell off his donkey," … "Riding in from
  the plain. Drunk as a Boeotian, and singing."
- **Terrace and street texture** — the woman along the wall at dawn ("That's her," … "She'll be going down now."); the rod-man calling the
  cities by turn ("The rod came out again and called Phocians."); **the Thessalian family** ("a black ram that would not climb, and three
  sons pushing it"; the ram's bolt is the jug boy's report only, never staged); **the island fig-seller** "swearing to each new face that
  they were Chian"; **the stall-keeper** below the altar, "sucking his fingers between sales" (the cakes "on trays with a laurel leaf burned
  into the wood of the rim"); **the clay-ear seller** with "a board of clay ears on a trestle"; **an Achaean** who "snored a yard from my
  mat"; **a yawning Thessalian** sent to the far trough; the street on the climb to Theano: "a woman carding wool on her step, a girl
  plucking a hen into a basket, an old fellow asleep upright against a wine jar with his chin on his chest and a kitten in his lap";
  **Theano's cat** ("That's the cat's.").
- **Mention only:** **Kleon** (Praxias's father); Pindar (unnamed, "A poet" … "a Boeotian", his iron chair); Homer (the measure only);
  Tarquinia and Volterra (Mara's father's slides); Etna, Etruria, Massalia, Spain; Capua's schools (Felix's sailor at Kirrha); Theano's late
  husband and her sister's son (the ewes and the cheese money); Felix's mother (once). **Spicula** is not named in Ch.13 (0); the goat is
  "the goat" throughout.
